Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

ButCoder Inc. runs a programming competition site called ButCoder. In this site, a user is given an integer value called rating that represents his/her skill, which changes each time he/she participates in a contest. The initial value of a new user's rating is 0, and a user whose rating reaches K or higher is called Kaiden ("total transmission"). Note that a user's rating may become negative.

Hikuhashi is a new user in ButCoder. It is estimated that, his rating increases by A in each of his odd-numbered contests (first, third, fifth, ...), and decreases by B in each of his even-numbered contests (second, fourth, sixth, ...).

According to this estimate, after how many contests will he become Kaiden for the first time, or will he never become Kaiden?

Constraints

* 1 ≤ K, A, B ≤ 10^{18}
* All input values are integers.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


K A B


Output

If it is estimated that Hikuhashi will never become Kaiden, print `-1`. Otherwise, print the estimated number of contests before he become Kaiden for the first time.

Examples

Input

4000 2000 500


Output

5


Input

4000 500 2000


Output

-1


Input

1000000000000000000 2 1


Output

1999999999999999997

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Hikuhashi starts with rating 0
2. In odd-numbered contests (1st, 3rd, 5th, ...): rating increases by A
3. In even-numbered contests (2nd, 4th, 6th, ...): rating decreases by B
4. We need to find the first contest where rating reaches K or higher, or determine if it's impossible

## Analysis

Let's trace through the pattern:
- After 1 contest: rating = A
- After 2 contests: rating = A - B
- After 3 contests: rating = A - B + A = 2A - B
- After 4 contests: rating = 2A - B - B = 2A - 2B
- After 5 contests: rating = 2A - 2B + A = 3A - 2B
- After 6 contests: rating = 3A - 2B - B = 3A - 3B

For an odd number of contests (2n+1):
- Rating = (n+1)A - nB

For an even number of contests (2n):
- Rating = nA - nB = n(A - B)

## Key Cases

**Case 1: A ≤ B**
- If A ≤ B, then in even-numbered contests, the rating decreases or stays the same
- After any even contest, rating = n(A - B) ≤ 0
- After odd contests, rating = (n+1)A - nB
- We need to check if this can exceed K

For odd contests at step 2n+1:
- Rating = (n+1)A - nB = (n+1)A - nB
- = n(A - B) + A
- Since A ≤ B, (A - B) ≤ 0, so n(A - B) ≤ 0
- So rating ≤ A

Therefore, if A ≤ B and A < K, then rating will never reach K.
If A ≤ B and A ≥ K, then after 1 contest rating = A ≥ K, so answer is 1.

**Case 2: A > B**
- The net gain per 2 contests is A - B > 0
- The pattern will eventually reach K
- We need to find the minimum n such that rating ≥ K

For odd number of contests 2n+1:
- Rating = (n+1)A - nB = n(A - B) + A
- We want n(A - B) + A ≥ K
- n(A - B) ≥ K - A
- n ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)

For even number of contests 2n:
- Rating = n(A - B)
- We want n(A - B) ≥ K
- n ≥ K / (A - B)

Since (A - B) > 0, we can find the minimum n for each case and take the minimum value.

But we need to be careful about the exact constraint.

Actually, let's reconsider:

After 2n+1 contests: rating = (n+1)A - nB
After 2n contests: rating = n(A - B)

We need to find the minimum number of contests where rating ≥ K.

If A ≤ B:
- After odd contest: rating = (n+1)A - nB = A - n(B - A)
- Since B - A ≥ 0, rating ≤ A
- So if A < K, rating will never reach K
- If A ≥ K, then after 1 contest, rating = A ≥ K

If A > B:
- After even contest 2n: rating = n(A - B)
- After odd contest 2n+1: rating = (n+1)A - nB = n(A - B) + A

So we need to check if at any point the rating reaches K.

For even contest 2n: we need n(A - B) ≥ K
For odd contest 2n+1: we need n(A - B) + A ≥ K

Since we want the first time, we need to find the smallest n such that either condition holds.

But note: for odd contests, the rate of increase is faster than even contests (since we add A instead of A-B).

So we should check both cases, but start from n=0.

Actually, let's be systematic:

1. If A ≤ B and A < K: impossible → return -1
2. If A ≤ B and A ≥ K: answer is 1 (after 1st contest)
3. If A > B: we can eventually reach K

For A > B, we need to find minimum number of contests.

For even contests 2n: rating = n(A - B)
For odd contests 2n+1: rating = (n+1)A - nB = n(A - B) + A

We need rating ≥ K.

The odd contest pattern has a higher rate of increase (we add A to the base of the even pattern).

So we should check if K can be reached at an even position or odd position.

We need to find the smallest m such that rating after m contests ≥ K.

For m even (m = 2n): n(A - B) ≥ K → n ≥ K / (A - B)
For m odd (m = 2n+1): n(A - B) + A ≥ K → n ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)

Since both are linear, we can find the minimum n that satisfies either.

But we need to be careful about when A ≤ B.

Wait, let me reconsider A ≤ B case:

If A ≤ B:
- After 1st contest: rating = A
- After 2nd contest: rating = A - B ≤ 0
- After 3rd contest: rating = A - B + A = 2A - B ≤ A (since A ≤ B)
- After 4th contest: rating = 2A - 2B ≤ 0
- And so on...

The maximum rating after any odd contest is A, after any even contest is ≤ 0.

So if A < K, rating will never reach K → return -1
If A ≥ K, rating reaches K after 1 contest → return 1

If A > B:
- The rating tends to increase over time
- After 2n contests: rating = n(A - B)
- After 2n+1 contests: rating = n(A - B) + A

We need to find the minimum m such that rating ≥ K.

Let's check the even case first:
- m = 2n, we need n(A - B) ≥ K → n ≥ K / (A - B) → m ≥ 2K / (A - B)

For odd case:
- m = 2n+1, we need n(A - B) + A ≥ K → n ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)
- If K ≤ A, then (K - A) ≤ 0, so n ≥ negative number → smallest n is 0 → m = 1
- If K > A, then n ≥ (K - A) / (A - B) → m ≥ 2(K - A) / (A - B) + 1

Since (K - A) / (A - B) ≤ K / (A - B) (because K - A ≤ K and A - B > 0), the odd case might be smaller or larger.

Actually, the odd case formula: n ≥ (K - A) / (A - B), so m = 2n + 1 ≥ 2 * max(0, (K - A) / (A - B)) + 1

But if K ≤ A, then after 1st contest we already have rating = A ≥ K, so answer is 1.

So the algorithm:

1. If A < K and A ≤ B: return -1 (can never reach K)
2. If A ≥ K: return 1 (after 1st contest)
3. If A > B and A < K: we need to find minimum m such that rating ≥ K

For even m = 2n: rating = n(A - B)
For odd m = 2n+1: rating = n(A - B) + A

We want the minimum m such that any of these is ≥ K.

For odd m: n(A - B) + A ≥ K → n ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)
Since A < K, (K - A) > 0, so n ≥ ceil((K - A) / (A - B))

Then m = 2n + 1 = 2 * ceil((K - A) / (A - B)) + 1

For even m: n(A - B) ≥ K → n ≥ ceil(K / (A - B))
Then m = 2n = 2 * ceil(K / (A - B))

We take the minimum of these two values.

But we also have the constraint that A > B.

Actually, let me verify with the example:
K=4000, A=2000, B=500
A > B (2000 > 500)
A < K (2000 < 4000)

For odd m = 2n+1: n ≥ (4000 - 2000) / (2000 - 500) = 2000 / 1500 = 1.333... → n = 2 → m = 5
For even m = 2n: n ≥ 4000 / 1500 = 2.666... → n = 3 → m = 6
Minimum is 5, which matches expected output.

Another example:
K=4000, A=500, B=2000
A < B, and A < K → should return -1, which matches.

Example 3:
K=1000000000000000000, A=2, B=1
A > B, A < K (2 < 1000000000000000000)

For odd m: n ≥ (K-A)/(A-B) = (1000000000000000000-2)/(2-1) = 999999999999999998
So n = 999999999999999998, m = 2*999999999999999998 + 1 = 1999999999999999997
For even m: n ≥ K/(A-B) = 1000000000000000000/1 = 1000000000000000000, m = 2*1000000000000000000 = 2000000000000000000
Minimum is 1999999999999999997, which matches.

So algorithm:

1. If A < K and A ≤ B: return -1
2. If A ≥ K: return 1
3. If A > B and A < K: 
   - Calculate n_odd = ceil((K - A) / (A - B))
   - m_odd = 2 * n_odd + 1
   - Calculate n_even = ceil(K / (A - B))  
   - m_even = 2 * n_even
   - Return min(m_odd, m_even)

For division by (A-B), we need to handle ceiling division.

Ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b for positive integers.

But note: (K-A) might be 0, but we already have A < K.

```python
import math

K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# If A is already >= K, we reach Kaiden after 1 contest
if A >= K:
    print(1)
# If A <= B, then after odd contests rating <= A, after even contests rating <= 0
# So if A < K, we never reach Kaiden
elif A <= B:
    print(-1)
else:
    # A > B and A < K
    # For odd-numbered contests: after 2n+1 contests, rating = n*(A-B) + A
    # We need n*(A-B) + A >= K → n*(A-B) >= K-A → n >= (K-A)/(A-B)
    # n_odd = ceil((K-A)/(A-B))
    n_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)
    m_odd = 2 * n_odd + 1
    
    # For even-numbered contests: after 2n contests, rating = n*(A-B)
    # We need n*(A-B) >= K → n >= K/(A-B)
    # n_even = ceil(K/(A-B))
    n_even = (K + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)
    m_even = 2 * n_even
    
    print(min(m_odd, m_even))
```

Wait, I think there's an error in the ceiling division.

For ceiling division: ceil(x/y) = (x + y - 1) // y when x and y are positive.

But in our case:
- (K-A) is positive (since K > A)
- (A-B) is positive (since A > B)

So:
- n_odd = ceil((K-A)/(A-B)) = (K-A + (A-B) - 1) // (A-B) = (K - B - 1) // (A-B)

Actually, let me verify:
ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b

So for n_odd: ceil((K-A)/(A-B)) = (K-A + (A-B) - 1) // (A-B) = (K - B - 1) // (A-B)

For n_even: ceil(K/(A-B)) = (K + (A-B) - 1) // (A-B) = (K + A - B - 1) // (A-B)

Let me test with the example:
K=4000, A=2000, B=500
A-B = 1500
K-A = 2000
n_odd = ceil(2000/1500) = ceil(1.333) = 2
Using formula: (2000 + 1500 - 1) // 1500 = 3499 // 1500 = 2 ✓

n_even = ceil(4000/1500) = ceil(2.666) = 3
Using formula: (4000 + 1500 - 1) // 1500 = 5499 // 1500 = 3 ✓

So the formulas are correct.

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# If A >= K, after 1st contest we reach Kaiden
if A >= K:
    print(1)
# If A <= B, then the rating cannot exceed A (after odd contests) or go positive after even contests
# So if A < K, we never reach Kaiden
elif A <= B:
    print(-1)
else:
    # A > B and A < K
    # For odd-numbered contests: after 2n+1 contests, rating = n*(A-B) + A >= K
    # So n >= (K-A)/(A-B), n_odd = ceil((K-A)/(A-B))
    n_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)
    m_odd = 2 * n_odd + 1
    
    # For even-numbered contests: after 2n contests, rating = n*(A-B) >= K
    # So n >= K/(A-B), n_even = ceil(K/(A-B))
    n_even = (K + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)
    m_even = 2 * n_even
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Hikuhashi starts with rating 0
2. In odd-numbered contests (1st, 3rd, 5th, ...): rating increases by A
3. In even-numbered contests (2nd, 4th, 6th, ...): rating decreases by B
4. We need to find the first contest where rating reaches K or higher, or determine if it's impossible

## Analysis

Let me trace through the pattern:
- After 1 contest: rating = A
- After 2 contests: rating = A - B
- After 3 contests: rating = A - B + A = 2A - B
- After 4 contests: rating = 2A - B - B = 2A - 2B
- After 5 contests: rating = 2A - 2B + A = 3A - 2B
- After 6 contests: rating = 3A - 2B - B = 3A - 3B

Pattern: After n contests, the rating is:
- If n is odd: ((n+1)/2) * A - (n-1)/2 * B
- If n is even: (n/2) * A - (n/2) * B

We can simplify:
- For odd n = 2k+1: (k+1) * A - k * B = k(A - B) + A
- For even n = 2k: k * A - k * B = k(A - B)

## Key Insight

The rating changes depend on whether A > B or A ≤ B:

**Case 1: A ≤ B**
- The rating will eventually decrease or stay the same
- After each complete cycle of 2 contests, the rating decreases by (B - A)
- If A < B, rating will keep decreasing after each pair
- If A = B, rating stays constant at 0 after each pair
- So if A ≤ B, Hikuhashi will never reach K (unless K ≤ 0, but K ≥ 1)

**Case 2: A > B**
- The rating keeps increasing over time
- After each pair of contests, the net gain is (A - B)
- After 2k contests: k(A - B)
- We need k(A - B) ≥ K, so k ≥ K / (A - B)
- The first time rating ≥ K will be at some odd or even contest

Let me check when rating first reaches K:

For odd contest n = 2k+1: rating = (k+1)A - kB = k(A-B) + A
We need k(A-B) + A ≥ K
So k ≥ (K - A) / (A-B)

For even contest n = 2k: rating = k(A-B)
We need k(A-B) ≥ K
So k ≥ K / (A-B)

Since k must be an integer, we need to find the minimum k such that either:
- k(A-B) ≥ K (even contest), or 
- k(A-B) + A ≥ K (odd contest)

- If n is even: (n/2)(A-B)

- n=1: 2
- n=2: 1
- n=3: 3*2 - 2*1 = 6-2 = 4? Wait no

For n=1: k=0, rate = 0*(2-1) + 2 = 2 ✓
For n=2: k=1, rate = 1*(2-1) = 1 ✓
For n=3: k=1, rate = 1*(2-1) + 2 = 1 + 2 = 3 ✓  
For n=4: k=2, rate = 2*(2-1) = 2 ✓
For n=5: k=2, rate = 2*(2-1) + 2 = 2 + 2 = 4 ✓

After 2k+1: rating = k(A-B) + A
After 2k: rating = k(A-B)

We want rating ≥ K = 10^18.

For odd: k(A-B) + A ≥ K → k ≥ (K-A)/(A-B)
k_min = ceil((K-A)/(A-B))
n_odd = 2*k_min + 1

For even: k(A-B) ≥ K → k ≥ K/(A-B)
k_min = ceil(K/(A-B))
n_even = 2*k_min

Then answer = min(n_odd, n_even)

For example 3: K = 10^18, A=2, B=1, A-B=1
- For odd: k ≥ (10^18 - 2)/1 = 10^18 - 2, so k_min = 10^18 - 2, n_odd = 2*(10^18 - 2) + 1 = 2*10^18 - 4 + 1 = 2*10^18 - 3
- For even: k ≥ 10^18 / 1 = 10^18, so k_min = 10^18, n_even = 2*10^18
- Answer = 2*10^18 - 3 = 1999999999999999997 ✓

Perfect!

So algorithm:
1. If A ≤ B: return -1
2. If A ≥ K: return 1
3. Else:
   - For odd contests: k_min = ceil((K-A)/(A-B)), n_odd = 2*k_min + 1
   - For even contests: k_min = ceil(K/(A-B)), n_even = 2*k_min
   - Return min(n_odd, n_even)

Now need to implement ceil division: ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b when a, b > 0

```python
import math

K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# If A <= B, rating will never reach K (since it decreases or stays same after each pair)
if A <= B:
    print(-1)
else:
    # If A >= K, after first contest rating becomes A >= K
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        # A > B and A < K
        # For odd contests: after 2k+1 contests, rating = k*(A-B) + A
        # Need k*(A-B) + A >= K, so k >= (K-A)/(A-B)
        # k_min_odd = ceil((K-A)/(A-B))
        k_min_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # This is wrong
        
        # Correct ceil division: ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b
        # But since (K-A) might be negative, but K > A so it's positive
        k_min_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # Still wrong
        
        # Correct: ceil(x/y) for positive x,y is (x + y - 1) // y
        k_min_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # This gives (K-A-1)//(A-B)
        
        # Actually: ceil((K-A)/(A-B)) = (K-A + (A-B) - 1) // (A-B) = (K - 1) // (A-B)
        # No: ceil(m/n) = (m + n - 1) // n
        # So ceil((K-A)/(A-B)) = (K-A + (A-B) - 1) // (A-B) = (K - 1) // (A-B)
        # Let me verify: if K-A=3, A-B=2, then ceil(3/2)=2, (3+2-1)//2 = 4//2 = 2 ✓
        
        k_min_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)
        n_odd = 2 * k_min_odd + 1
        
        # For even contests: after 2k contests, rating = k*(A-B)
        # Need k*(A-B) >= K, so k >= K/(A-B)
        k_min_even = (K + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # ceil(K/(A-B))
        n_even = 2 * k_min_even
        
        print(min(n_odd, n_even))
```

Wait, let me verify the ceiling formula:
- ceil(m/n) = (m + n - 1) // n for positive integers m,n

For odd: m = K-A, n = A-B
- k_min_odd = (K-A + (A-B) - 1) // (A-B) = (K - B - 1) // (A-B)

- k_min_odd = (10^18 - 1 - 1) // 1 = 10^18 - 2
- n_odd = 2*(10^18 - 2) + 1 = 2*10^18 - 3 ✓

For even: m = K, n = A-B=1
- k_min_even = (10^18 + 1 - 1) // 1 = 10^18
- n_even = 2*10^18

min = 2*10^18 - 3 ✓

But is the formula correct? (K-A + (A-B) - 1) // (A-B) = (K - B - 1) // (A-B)

Yes, that's correct.

Actually: (K-A) + (A-B) - 1 = K - A + A - B - 1 = K - B - 1

So it's (K - B - 1) // (A - B)

K-A=2000

Formula: (4000 - 500 - 1) // 1500 = 3499 // 1500 = 2 ✓

In this site, a user is given an integer value called rating that represents his/her skill, which changes each time he/she participates in a contest. The initial value of a new user's rating is 0, and a user whose rating reaches K or higher is called Kaiden ("total transmission"). Note that a user's rating may become negative.

Hikuhashi is a new user in ButCoder. It is estimated that, his rating increases by A in each of his odd-numbered contests (first, third, fifth, ...), and decreases by B in each of his even-numbered contests (second, fourth, sixth, ...).

According to this estimate, after how many contests will he become Kaiden for the first time, or will he never become Kaiden?

Constraints

* 1 ≤ K, A, B ≤ 10^{18}
* All input values are integers.

K A B

```python
                # But we need to ensure n_odd >= 1
                n_odd = max(1, (K - B + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B))
                # Actually, ceiling of x is (x + denominator - 1) // denominator
                n_odd = (K - B + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)
                if n_odd < 0:
                    n_odd = 0
                n_odd = max(1, n_odd)
                
                # This gives us the n for the odd case (2n-1 contests)
                # But we also have even case
                # For even case: n >= K / (A - B)
                n_even = (K + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # ceiling division
                n_even = max(1, n_even)
                
                # The number of contests for odd case is 2*n_odd - 1
                # For even case is 2*n_even
                # We want the minimum
                contests_odd = 2 * n_odd - 1
                contests_even = 2 * n_even
                
                if contests_odd < contests_even:
                    print(contests_odd)
                else:
                    print(contests_even)
        pass
    else:
        # A > B and K > B
        # Solve n >= (K - B) / (A - B) for odd case
        # and n >= K / (A - B) for even case
        import math
        
        # For odd case after (2n-1) contests
        # n >= (K - B) / (A - B)
        # ceiling of (K - B) / (A - B)
        if (K - B) % (A - B) == 0:
            n_odd = (K - B) // (A - B)
        else:
            n_odd = (K - B) // (A - B) + 1
        
        if n_odd <= 0:
            n_odd = 1
            
        contests_odd = 2 * n_odd - 1
        
        # For even case after 2n contests
        if K % (A - B) == 0:
            n_even = K // (A - B)
        else:
            n_even = K // (A - B) + 1
            
        if n_even <= 0:
            n_even = 1
            
        contests_even = 2 * n_even
        
        # We want the minimum number of contests
        if contests_odd < contests_even:
            print(contests_odd)
        else:
            print(contests_even)
```

I think I'm making this too complicated. Let me restart with a cleaner approach.

## Clean Solution

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

if A <= B:
    # In this case, after each pair of contests, rating decreases or stays the same
    # The rating after odd contests (1st, 3rd, 5th, ...) is:
    # after 1 contest: A
    # after 3 contests: 2A - B
    # after 5 contests: 3A - 2B
    # after (2n-1) contests: nA - (n-1)B
    
    # Since A <= B, this sequence is non-increasing
    # So we only need to check the values until we find one >= K
    # But we can also see that if A < K, then after first contest we have A < K
    # and the sequence decreases, so never reaches K
    
    # If A >= K, then after 1 contest we reach K
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        # Check for larger odd contests
        # We need nA - (n-1)B >= K
        # => n(A-B) + B >= K
        # Since A-B <= 0, and K > A >= 0, we need to see if there exists n such that this holds
        
        # If A-B < 0 (A < B), then n(A-B) + B is decreasing in n
        # The maximum value is at n=1: A < K, so never reaches K
        
        # If A-B = 0 (A = B), then rating after any odd contest is always A < K, so never reaches K
        
        print(-1)
else:
    # A > B, rating increases over time
    # After odd contest (2n-1): rating = nA - (n-1)B = n(A-B) + B
    # We want n(A-B) + B >= K
    # => n >= (K - B) / (A - B)
    
    # After even contest (2n): rating = n(A-B)
    # We want n(A-B) >= K
    # => n >= K / (A - B)
    
    # The number of contests for odd case: 2n - 1
    # For even case: 2n
    
    # We want the minimum number of contests
    
    # First, handle the case where (K - B) / (A - B) >= 0
    if K > B:
        # Both cases need to be considered
        import math
        
        # For odd case: n >= (K - B) / (A - B)
        # n_odd = ceil((K - B) / (A - B))
        if (K - B) % (A - B) == 0:
            n_odd = (K - B) // (A - B)
        else:
            n_odd = (K - B) // (A - B) + 1
        # Minimum n for odd case
        n_odd = max(1, n_odd)
        contests_odd = 2 * n_odd - 1
        
        # For even case: n >= K / (A - B)
        if K % (A - B) == 0:
            n_even = K // (A - B)
        else:
            n_even = K // (A - B) + 1
        n_even = max(1, n_even)
        contests_even = 2 * n_even
        
        print(min(contests_odd, contests_even))
    else:
        # K <= B
        # For odd case: n >= (K - B) / (A - B)
        # Since K <= B, (K - B) <= 0, and (A - B) > 0, so (K - B)/(A-B) <= 0
        # So n >= 0, so minimum n is 1
        # After 1 contest: rating = A > B >= K, so A > K
        # Therefore, we reach K at contest 1
        print(1)
```

Wait, I need to handle the case where K <= B more carefully. If K <= B and A > B, then A > B >= K, so after 1 contest rating = A > K, so answer is 1.

Let me write the final clean solution:

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

if A <= B:
    # If A >= K, after 1 contest we reach K
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        # After first odd contest: A < K
        # Since A <= B, the rating after subsequent odd contests will be <= A (or equal if A=B)
        # Therefore, never reaches K
        print(-1)
else:
    # A > B, rating increases over time
    # Case 1: K <= B
    # After 1 contest: rating = A > B >= K, so reaches K at 1st contest
    if K <= B:
        print(1)
    else:
        # K > B
        # We need to find minimum n such that either:
        # In odd case (2n-1 contests): n(A-B) + B >= K
        # In even case (2n contests): n(A-B) >= K
        
        # Solving for odd case: n >= (K - B) / (A - B)
        # Solving for even case: n >= K / (A - B)
        
        # For odd case
        n_odd = (K - B + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # ceiling division
        if n_odd <= 0:
            n_odd = 1
        contests_odd = 2 * n_odd - 1
        
        # For even case
        n_even = (K + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # ceiling division
        if n_even <= 0:
            n_even = 1
        contests_even = 2 * n_even
        
        print(min(contests_odd, contests_even))
```

But wait, the ceiling division formula might not be perfect. Let me use proper ceiling division.

Actually, for positive integers, ceiling(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b when a, b > 0.

But in our case, for odd case: we need n >= (K-B)/(A-B). Since K>B and A>B, both are positive.

For even case: n >= K/(A-B), both positive.

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

if A <= B:
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        print(-1)
else:
    # A > B
    if K <= B:
        print(1)
    else:
        # K > B
        # Odd case: n >= (K - B) / (A - B)
        # Even case: n >= K / (A - B)
        diff = A - B
        n_odd = (K - B + diff - 1) // diff
        n_even = (K + diff - 1) // diff
        
        contests_odd = 2 * n_odd - 1
        contests_even = 2 * n_even
        
        print(min(contests_odd, contests_even))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Hikuhashi starts with rating 0
2. In odd-numbered contests (1st, 3rd, 5th, ...): rating increases by A
3. In even-numbered contests (2nd, 4th, 6th, ...): rating decreases by B
4. We need to find after how many contests his rating first reaches K or higher
5. If he never reaches K, return -1

## Analysis

Let's think about the pattern:
- After 1 contest: rating = A
- After 2 contests: rating = A - B
- After 3 contests: rating = A - B + A = 2A - B
- After 4 contests: rating = 2A - B - B = 2A - 2B
- After 5 contests: rating = 2A - 2B + A = 3A - 2B
- After 6 contests: rating = 3A - 2B - B = 3A - 3B

Pattern: After n contests:
- If n is odd: rating = ((n+1)//2) * A - ((n-1)//2) * B
- If n is even: rating = (n//2) * A - (n//2) * B

Simplifying:
- For odd n = 2k+1: rating = (k+1) * A - k * B = k*(A-B) + A
- For even n = 2k: rating = k * (A - B)

## Key Insights

1. **Case 1: A ≤ B**
   - If A ≤ B, then A - B ≤ 0
   - After each pair of contests (2k), rating = k * (A - B) ≤ 0
   - After odd contests, rating = k*(A-B) + A
   - Since A-B ≤ 0, the rating will either stay the same or decrease
   - Eventually, the rating will decrease or stay at a maximum of A (if A > 0)
   - If A < K, he will never reach K, so answer is -1
   - If A ≥ K, he reaches K at contest 1

2. **Case 2: A > B**
   - A - B > 0, so the rating increases over time
   - For even n = 2k: rating = k * (A - B)
   - For odd n = 2k+1: rating = k*(A-B) + A
   - The maximum rating in each pair increases
   - Since A > B, eventually the rating will exceed K

## Algorithm

1. If A ≥ K, answer is 1 (he reaches K in first contest)
2. If A ≤ B:
   - If A < K, answer is -1 (he will never reach K)
   - If A = K, answer is 1
3. If A > B:
   - We need to find the minimum n such that rating ≥ K
   - For even n = 2k: rating = k * (A - B)
   - For odd n = 2k+1: rating = k * (A - B) + A
   - We can solve for k in both cases
   - For even: k * (A - B) ≥ K → k ≥ K / (A - B)
   - For odd: k * (A - B) + A ≥ K → k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)
   - We need to check which gives the smaller n

Wait, let me reconsider the odd case:
- For odd n = 2k+1: rating = k*(A-B) + A
- We need k*(A-B) + A ≥ K
- k*(A-B) ≥ K - A
- k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)

Since we want the first time, we should check both even and odd cases.

Actually, let me think more systematically:

After 2k contests: rating = k * (A - B)
After 2k+1 contests: rating = k * (A - B) + A

Since A > B, the rating increases with k.

We can find:
- For even: k * (A - B) ≥ K → k ≥ K / (A - B)
- For odd: k * (A - B) + A ≥ K → k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)

But we also need to ensure k ≥ 0.

For the even case, k_even = ceil(K / (A - B))
For the odd case, k_odd = ceil(max(0, (K - A)) / (A - B))

Then n_even = 2 * k_even
n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1

Answer is min(n_even, n_odd)

But we also need to handle the case where A < K.

Actually, let me simplify:

1. If A ≥ K: return 1 (after 1st contest)
2. If A < K and A ≤ B: return -1 (never reaches K)
3. If A < K and A > B: 
   - Find the minimum n such that rating >= K
   - We can solve for k in the odd case: k*(A-B) + A >= K
   - k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
   - k_min = ceil((K - A) / (A - B))

We need k*(A-B) + A >= K, so k >= (K-A)/(A-B)
n_odd = 2k+1 >= 2*(K-A)/(A-B) + 1

Since A < K, (K-A) > 0, so both k_min values are positive.

However, let me verify with the examples:

For even: k*(2000-500) >= 4000 → k*1500 >= 4000 → k >= 4000/1500 ≈ 2.67 → k=3 → n=6
For odd: k*1500 + 2000 >= 4000 → k*1500 >= 2000 → k >= 2000/1500 ≈ 1.33 → k=2 → n=5
Answer should be 5. ✓

Example 2: K=4000, A=500, B=2000
A < B, A < K → answer should be -1. ✓

Example 3: K=1000000000000000000, A=2, B=1
A > B, A < K
For even: k*(2-1) >= 10^18 → k >= 10^18 → n = 2*10^18
For odd: k*(2-1) + 2 >= 10^18 → k >= 10^18 - 2 → k = 10^18 - 2 → n = 2*(10^18 - 2) + 1 = 2*10^18 - 3
Wait, this gives n = 2*10^18 - 3, but expected is 1999999999999999997 = 2*10^18 - 3. ✓

Actually, let me recalculate example 3:

So the algorithm is:

1. If A >= K: return 1
2. If A <= B: return -1  
3. Else (A > B):
   - Calculate k_for_even = ceil(K / (A - B))
   - Calculate k_for_odd = ceil((K - A) / (A - B))
   - n_even = 2 * k_for_even
   - n_odd = 2 * k_for_odd + 1
   - return min(n_even, n_odd)

But I need to be careful with integer division and ceiling.

For positive integers: ceil(x/y) = (x + y - 1) // y

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# If A >= K, he reaches K in first contest
if A >= K:
    print(1)
# If A <= B, rating never increases enough to reach K
elif A <= B:
    print(-1)
else:
    # A > B and A < K
    # A - B > 0
    diff = A - B
    
    # For even number of contests: 2k
    # After 2k contests: rating = k * (A - B)
    # We need k * (A - B) >= K
    # k >= K / (A - B)
    k_even = (K + diff - 1) // diff  # This is ceil(K / diff)
    n_even = 2 * k_even
    
    # For odd number of contests: 2k + 1
    # After 2k+1 contests: rating = k * (A - B) + A
    # We need k * (A - B) + A >= K
    # k * (A - B) >= K - A
    # k >= (K - A) / (A - B) if K > A, else k >= 0
    if K <= A:
        # This shouldn't happen since we already checked A < K
        k_odd = 0
    else:
        k_odd = (K - A + diff - 1) // diff  # ceil((K-A)/(A-B))
    n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1
    
    print(min(n_even, n_odd))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Hikuhashi starts with rating 0
2. In odd-numbered contests (1st, 3rd, 5th, ...): rating increases by A
3. In even-numbered contests (2nd, 4th, 6th, ...): rating decreases by B
4. We need to find the first contest when rating reaches K or higher
5. If it's impossible to reach K, return -1

## Analysis

Let's trace through the pattern:
- After 1 contest: rating = A
- After 2 contests: rating = A - B  
- After 3 contests: rating = A - B + A = 2A - B
- After 4 contests: rating = 2A - B - B = 2A - 2B
- After 5 contests: rating = 2A - 2B + A = 3A - 2B
- After 6 contests: rating = 3A - 2B - B = 3A - 3B

So for n contests:
- If n is odd: rating = ((n+1)/2) * A - ((n-1)/2) * B
- If n is even: rating = (n/2) * A - (n/2) * B = (n/2) * (A - B)

## Key Insights

1. **When will it never become Kaiden?**
   - If A ≤ B, then after each pair of contests (odd + even), the rating decreases or stays the same.
   - Specifically, after 2 contests: rating = A - B ≤ 0
   - After 4 contests: rating = 2(A - B) ≤ 0
   - So if A ≤ B, the rating will never increase beyond 0, so it will never reach K (since K ≥ 1)

2. **When will it become Kaiden?**
   - If A > B, then the rating will eventually increase.
   - We need to find the minimum n such that rating ≥ K.

3. **Case analysis:**
   - If A ≤ B: return -1 (never becomes Kaiden)
   - If A > B: we need to find the smallest n such that rating ≥ K

## Finding the minimum n

For odd n = 2k+1:
- Rating = (k+1) * A - k * B = k(A - B) + A

For even n = 2k:
- Rating = k * (A - B)

We need to check when the rating first reaches K.

Since A > B, the rating increases with each pair of contests.

For even number of contests (n = 2k):
- Rating = k * (A - B)
- We need k * (A - B) ≥ K
- So k ≥ K / (A - B)
- n = 2k ≥ 2K / (A - B)

For odd number of contests (n = 2k+1):
- Rating = k(A - B) + A
- We need k(A - B) + A ≥ K
- So k(A - B) ≥ K - A
- k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)
- n = 2k+1 ≥ 2(K - A) / (A - B) + 1

We need to find the minimum n over all cases.

However, since the pattern is periodic with period 2, we can check:
1. First, check if it's possible (A > B)
2. Find when the rating first reaches K

Actually, let's think differently:

The rating after n contests:
- After 2k contests: k(A - B)
- After 2k+1 contests: k(A - B) + A

We need the minimum n such that this value ≥ K.

## Algorithm

1. If A ≤ B: return -1 (never becomes Kaiden)
2. If A > B: 
   - Check if A ≥ K: then after 1 contest, rating = A ≥ K, so answer = 1
   - Otherwise, we need to find minimum n > 1

For even n = 2k:
- We need k(A - B) ≥ K
- k ≥ K / (A - B)
- n = 2k ≥ 2K / (A - B)

For odd n = 2k+1:
- We need k(A - B) + A ≥ K
- k(A - B) ≥ K - A
- k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)
- n = 2k+1 ≥ 2(K - A) / (A - B) + 1

Since A > B, A - B > 0.

We need the minimum n. Let's check if the even case gives a smaller n than the odd case.

Actually, since A ≥ 0 and K > 0, the odd case might give a smaller n.

But let's simplify: we can calculate when the rating reaches K.

For the first few contests:
- n=1: rating = A
- n=2: rating = A - B
- n=3: rating = 2A - B
- n=4: rating = 2A - 2B
- n=5: rating = 3A - 2B
- n=6: rating = 3A - 3B

We can check if any small n satisfies the condition.

Since A > B, the rating increases with each pair of contests.

The rating after (k+1) pairs (i.e., after 2k+1 contests) is (k+1)A - kB.

We can binary search or directly compute.

Actually, let's derive the formula:

For odd n = 2k+1:
- Rating = k(A - B) + A
- We need k(A - B) + A ≥ K
- k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)

For even n = 2k:
- Rating = k(A - B)
- We need k(A - B) ≥ K
- k ≥ K / (A - B)

So the minimum n is:
- min(2k+1, 2k) where k is the smallest integer satisfying either condition.

Since k must be at least ceil((K-A)/(A-B)) for odd and ceil(K/(A-B)) for even, we need to check which gives smaller n.

But actually, since (A-B) > 0, and we're taking ceiling, the values are positive.

Let me implement this directly:

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# If A <= B, rating will never increase beyond initial value
if A <= B:
    print(-1)
else:
    # A > B, so rating will eventually increase
    # We need to find the smallest n such that rating >= K
    
    # After 1 contest: rating = A
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        # We need to find n >= 2
        # After n contests, the rating depends on whether n is even or odd
        
        # For even n = 2k: rating = k * (A - B)
        # For odd n = 2k+1: rating = k * (A - B) + A
        
        # For even n: k >= K / (A - B)
        # So n = 2k >= 2 * ceil(K / (A - B))
        
        # For odd n: k * (A - B) + A >= K
        # k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
        # So n = 2k + 1 >= 2 * ceil((K - A) / (A - B)) + 1
        
        # We need to compute these values
        diff = A - B
        
        # For even case: n_even = 2 * ceil(K / diff)
        # For odd case: n_odd = 2 * ceil((K - A) / diff) + 1
        
        # We need to compute ceiling of division
        def ceil_div(x, y):
            return (x + y - 1) // y if x > 0 else 0
        
        # For even case
        k_even = ceil_div(K, diff)
        n_even = 2 * k_even
        
        # For odd case
        if K > A:
            k_odd = ceil_div(K - A, diff)
            n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1
        else:
            # K <= A, but we already handled A >= K above
            n_odd = float('inf')
        
        # The answer is the minimum of even and odd cases
        # But we already know A >= K at start, so K <= A
        # So the odd case is valid
        
        n_odd = 2 * ceil_div(K - A, diff) + 1
        
        # Actually, we need to check if K <= A
        # If K <= A, we already have answer 1, so we don't need to compute here
        # In this branch, we have A < K, so K > A
        
        # We need min(n_even, n_odd)
        answer = min(n_even, n_odd)
        
        print(answer)
```

Wait, I made a mistake. If A >= K, answer is 1. Otherwise (A < K), we need to compute.

But let me reconsider: if A < K, then we need more than 1 contest.

Also, there's an issue with the formula for odd case. For odd n=2k+1, rating = k(A-B) + A.

We need k(A-B) + A >= K, so k >= (K-A)/(A-B).

Since we're dealing with integers, k >= ceil((K-A)/(A-B)).

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

if A <= B:
    print(-1)
else:
    # A > B
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        diff = A - B
        
        # For even n = 2k: rating = k * (A - B)
        # Need k * (A - B) >= K
        # k >= K / (A - B)
        k_even = (K + diff - 1) // diff  # ceiling division
        n_even = 2 * k_even
        
        # For odd n = 2k+1: rating = k * (A - B) + A
        # Need k * (A - B) + A >= K
        # k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
        # Since K > A, (K-A) > 0
        numerator_odd = K - A
        k_odd = (numerator_odd + diff - 1) // diff  # ceiling division
        n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1
        
        answer = min(n_even, n_odd)
        print(answer)
```

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: K=4000, A=2000, B=500
- A > B, so continue
- A < K, so compute
- diff = 1500
- k_even = ceil(4000/1500) = ceil(2.666) = 3
- n_even = 6
- k_odd = ceil((4000-2000)/1500) = ceil(2000/1500) = ceil(1.333) = 2
- n_odd = 2*2+1 = 5
- min(6,5) = 5 ✓

Example 2: K=4000, A=500, B=2000
- A <= B, so output -1 ✓

Example 3: K=1000000000000000000, A=2, B=1
- A > B
- A < K, so compute
- diff = 1
- k_even = ceil(K/1) = K
- n_even = 2K = 2000000000000000000
- k_odd = ceil((K-2)/1) = K-2
- n_odd = 2*(K-2) + 1 = 2K - 3 = 1999999999999999997 ✓

Perfect! The solution works.

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

if A <= B:
    print(-1)
else:
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        diff = A - B
        
        # For even number of contests: n = 2k, rating = k * (A - B)
        # Need k * (A - B) >= K
        k_even = (K + diff - 1) // diff  # ceiling division
        n_even = 2 * k_even
        
        # For odd number of contests: n = 2k + 1, rating = k * (A - B) + A
        # Need k * (A - B) + A >= K
        # k * (A - B) >= K - A
        k_odd = (K - A + diff - 1) // diff  # ceiling division
        n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1
        
        print(min(n_even, n_odd))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Hikuhashi starts with rating 0
2. In odd-numbered contests (1st, 3rd, 5th, ...): rating increases by A
3. In even-numbered contests (2nd, 4th, 6th, ...): rating decreases by B
4. We need to find when his rating first reaches K (or if it never reaches K)

## Analysis

Let me trace through the pattern:
- After 1 contest: rating = A
- After 2 contests: rating = A - B
- After 3 contests: rating = A - B + A = 2A - B
- After 4 contests: rating = 2A - B - B = 2A - 2B
- After 5 contests: rating = 2A - 2B + A = 3A - 2B
- After 6 contests: rating = 3A - 2B - B = 3A - 3B

Pattern: After n contests, rating = ⌈n/2⌉ × A - ⌊n/2⌋ × B

Let me define:
- If n is odd: n = 2k+1, rating = (k+1)A - k×B = A + k(A-B)
- If n is even: n = 2k, rating = kA - k×B = k(A-B)

## Cases to consider:

**Case 1: A ≤ B**
- After even number of contests: rating = k(A-B) ≤ 0 (since A-B ≤ 0)
- After odd number of contests: rating = A + k(A-B) ≤ A (since A-B ≤ 0)
- The maximum rating is A (after 1st contest)
- If A < K, then Hikuhashi never reaches K
- If A ≥ K, then he reaches K at the first contest

**Case 2: A > B**
- After even contests: rating = k(A-B) → increases without bound
- After odd contests: rating = A + k(A-B) → also increases without bound
- We need to find the minimum n such that rating ≥ K

## Finding when rating ≥ K:

For odd n = 2k+1:
rating = (k+1)A - kB = A + k(A-B)
We want A + k(A-B) ≥ K
k(A-B) ≥ K-A
k ≥ (K-A)/(A-B)

For even n = 2k:
rating = k(A-B)
We want k(A-B) ≥ K
k ≥ K/(A-B)

Since A > B, both cases are valid.

The minimum n comes from either odd or even positions.

## Step-by-step solution:

1. If A ≤ B:
   - If A < K: never reaches K → return -1
   - If A ≥ K: reaches K at 1st contest → return 1

2. If A > B:
   - We need to check both even and odd cases
   - For even n = 2k: k ≥ K/(A-B), so n = 2k ≥ 2K/(A-B)
   - For odd n = 2k+1: k ≥ (K-A)/(A-B), so n = 2k+1 ≥ 2(K-A)/(A-B) + 1
   - We need the minimum n satisfying either condition

But we need to be careful with the case when A ≤ K.

Actually, let me reconsider: if A > B, then the rating will eventually grow without bound, so he will become Kaiden.

We need to find the minimum n such that rating ≥ K.

For even n = 2k: rating = k(A-B) ≥ K → k ≥ K/(A-B) → n = 2k ≥ 2K/(A-B)
For odd n = 2k+1: rating = A + k(A-B) ≥ K → k ≥ (K-A)/(A-B)

Since we want the minimum n, we need to check both cases and take the minimum valid n.

However, if A < K, we still need to check if he can reach K at some point.

Actually, if A ≤ B, then the rating after the first contest is A, and after subsequent contests it alternates. If A < K, and A ≤ B, then the maximum rating achievable is A, so he never reaches K.

If A > B, then the rating grows over time, so he will eventually reach K.

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: K=4000, A=2000, B=500
A > B, so he will reach K.
After 1 contest: 2000
After 2: 2000-500 = 1500
After 3: 1500+2000 = 3500
After 4: 3500-500 = 3000
After 5: 3000+2000 = 5000 ≥ 4000 ✓

So he becomes Kaiden at contest 5.

Let me calculate for odd n=2k+1:
k ≥ (K-A)/(A-B) = (4000-2000)/(2000-500) = 2000/1500 = 1.333...
So k ≥ 2 (since k must be integer), so k=2, n=2*2+1 = 5 ✓

For even n=2k:
k ≥ K/(A-B) = 4000/1500 = 2.666...
So k ≥ 3, n = 6.

Minimum is 5.

Example 2: K=4000, A=500, B=2000
A < B, so the rating after first contest is 500, after second is 500-2000 = -1500, then 1500, etc.

After 1: 500
After 2: 500-2000 = -1500
After 3: -1500+500 = -1000
After 4: -1000-2000 = -3000
After 5: -3000+500 = -2500

The rating is decreasing in the long run. After odd contests, rating is decreasing (since A-B < 0), and after even contests it's also decreasing.

Maximum rating is 500. Since 500 < 4000, he never reaches K → return -1.

So the solution:

1. If A ≤ B:
   - If A < K: never reaches → return -1
   - If A ≥ K: reaches at 1st contest → return 1

2. If A > B:
   - For odd n = 2k+1: we need A + k(A-B) ≥ K → k ≥ (K-A)/(A-B)
   - For even n = 2k: we need k(A-B) ≥ K → k ≥ K/(A-B)
   - Find minimum n in both cases and return the smaller one

But we need to handle integer arithmetic carefully.

For odd case:
k ≥ ceil((K-A)/(A-B)) if (K-A) > 0, else k ≥ 0
Actually k ≥ maximum(0, ceil((K-A)/(A-B)))

For even case:
k ≥ ceil(K/(A-B))

```python
import math

K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# If A <= B, the rating won't grow to K (or will peak at A)
# So if A < K, he never becomes Kaiden
if A <= B:
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        print(-1)
else:
    # A > B, rating will eventually grow
    # For even n = 2k: rating = k * (A - B) >= K
    # k >= K / (A - B)
    # n = 2k >= 2 * K / (A - B)
    
    # For odd n = 2k+1: rating = A + k * (A - B) >= K
    # k * (A - B) >= K - A
    # k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
    # n = 2k + 1 >= 2 * (K - A) / (A - B) + 1
    
    # Calculate minimum k for even case
    # k_even >= K / (A - B)
    k_even = (K + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # ceiling of K / (A - B)
    n_even = 2 * k_even
    
    # Calculate minimum k for odd case
    # k_odd >= max(0, (K - A) / (A - B))
    if K <= A:
        # When K <= A, we can achieve K at n=1
        k_odd = 0
        n_odd = 1
    else:
        # K > A, so we need (K - A) / (A - B)
        k_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # ceiling of (K-A) / (A-B)
        n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1
    
    # Return the minimum of even and odd cases
    result = min(n_even, n_odd)
    print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful. For the odd case when K <= A, he reaches K at n=1, which is valid.

Actually, let me reconsider the odd case formula:
After odd contest n=2k+1, rating = A + k*(A-B)

When k=0, rating = A
So if A >= K, he reaches K at n=1.

If A < K, we need k such that A + k*(A-B) >= K
k*(A-B) >= K-A
Since A-B > 0, k >= (K-A)/(A-B)

So for both cases, we need to consider k >= max(0, ceil((K-A)/(A-B)))

But if K <= A, then (K-A) <= 0, so we don't need k > 0. We can achieve it with k=0, which gives n=1.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Hikuhashi starts with rating 0
2. In odd-numbered contests (1st, 3rd, 5th, ...): rating increases by A
3. In even-numbered contests (2nd, 4th, 6th, ...): rating decreases by B
4. We need to find the first contest when rating reaches K or more
5. If it's impossible, return -1

## Analysis

Let me trace through the pattern:

- After 1 contest: rating = A
- After 2 contests: rating = A - B
- After 3 contests: rating = A - B + A = 2A - B
- After 4 contests: rating = 2A - B - B = 2A - 2B
- After 5 contests: rating = 2A - 2B + A = 3A - 2B
- After 6 contests: rating = 3A - 2B - B = 3A - 3B

Pattern: After n contests:
- If n is odd: rating = ((n+1)/2) * A - ((n-1)/2) * B
- If n is even: rating = (n/2) * A - (n/2) * B

We can simplify this:
- For odd n = 2k+1: rating = (k+1) * A - k * B = k(A - B) + A
- For even n = 2k: rating = k * A - k * B = k(A - B)

## Key Insights

1. If A ≤ B, then after each 2-contest cycle, the rating doesn't increase (it decreases or stays the same). 
   - After even number of contests: rating = k(A - B) ≤ 0 (since A ≤ B)
   - After odd number of contests: rating = k(A - B) + A ≤ A (which may be positive but not growing)
   - So if A ≤ B and A < K, it's impossible to reach K

2. If A > B, then the rating increases over time in a predictable pattern.

3. For A > B:
   - After 2k contests: rating = k(A - B)
   - After 2k+1 contests: rating = k(A - B) + A
   
   Since A > B, the rating keeps increasing.

## Solution Strategy

1. If A ≤ B:
   - After any even number of contests: rating = k(A - B) ≤ 0
   - After any odd number of contests: rating = k(A - B) + A
   - For the rating to reach K, we need k(A - B) + A ≥ K
   - But since A - B ≤ 0, k(A - B) → -∞ as k increases
   - The maximum rating after odd contests is A (when k=0)
   - So if A < K, it's impossible → return -1
   - If A ≥ K, then after 1st contest he becomes Kaiden → return 1

2. If A > B:
   - The rating keeps increasing over time
   - We need to find the smallest n such that rating ≥ K

Let me reconsider the odd case more carefully:

For odd n = 2k + 1:
- rating = k(A - B) + A

For even n = 2k:
- rating = k(A - B)

We need to check both cases.

The sequence of ratings:
- After 1: A
- After 2: A - B
- After 3: 2A - B
- After 4: 2A - 2B
- After 5: 3A - 2B
- After 6: 3A - 3B

So for even n = 2k: rating = k(A - B)
For odd n = 2k+1: rating = (k+1)A - kB

Since A > B, both sequences are increasing.

We need to find the smallest n such that rating ≥ K.

For a given k, we can check:
- After 2k contests: rating = k(A - B)
- After 2k+1 contests: rating = (k+1)A - kB

Since A > B, for large enough k, both will exceed K.

We can solve for when (k+1)A - kB ≥ K:
(k+1)A - kB ≥ K
k(A - B) + A ≥ K
k(A - B) ≥ K - A
k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)

Since k ≥ 0, if K - A ≤ 0 (i.e., A ≥ K), then k = 0 works.

If K > A, then k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)

We need to check both odd and even cases.

Actually, since (k+1)A - kB grows faster than k(A - B) when A > B, we should check the odd cases first.

But let me think about when it's impossible:

If A ≤ B and A < K, then after any odd contest the rating maxes out at A, and after even contests it goes negative. So impossible.

If A ≤ B and A ≥ K, then after 1st contest he reaches A ≥ K, so answer is 1.

If A > B, then eventually he will reach K.

So:
- If A ≤ B:
  - If A < K: return -1
  - If A ≥ K: return 1
- If A > B:
  - We need to find minimum n such that rating ≥ K

For odd n = 2k+1: rating = k(A-B) + A
For even n = 2k: rating = k(A-B)

Let me solve for when k(A-B) + A ≥ K:
k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B)

Since k must be integer ≥ 0:
k_min = max(0, ceil((K - A) / (A - B)))

Then n = 2k_min + 1

For even n = 2k: we need k(A-B) ≥ K
k ≥ K / (A - B)

k_even_min = ceil(K / (A - B))
n_even = 2 * k_even_min

We need the minimum of these two cases.

But for k_min from odd case: (K-A)/(A-B)
Since A > B, A-B > 0.

Let me check with example: K=4000, A=2000, B=500
A > B, so possible.

Odd case: k(A-B) + A ≥ 4000
k(1500) + 2000 ≥ 4000
k ≥ 2000/1500 = 1.333...
k ≥ 2 (since k must be integer)
k = 2, n = 2*2 + 1 = 5

Even case: k(1500) ≥ 4000
k ≥ 4000/1500 = 2.666...
k ≥ 3, n = 6

Minimum is 5. ✓

Another example: K=4000, A=500, B=2000
A < B, so A ≤ B and A < K, so return -1. ✓

One more: K=1000000000000000000, A=2, B=1
A > B, so possible.

Odd case: k(1) + 2 ≥ K
k ≥ K - 2  
k ≥ 999999999999999998
n = 2k + 1 = 2*(K-2) + 1 = 2K - 3 = 2*1000000000000000000 - 3 = 1999999999999999997 ✓

Wait, let me double check:
k ≥ (K - A) / (A - B) = (K - 2) / 1 = K - 2
k = K - 2
n = 2*(K-2) + 1 = 2K - 3

Yes, that's correct.

So algorithm:

1. If A <= B:
   - if A >= K: return 1
   - else: return -1
2. If A > B:
   - Find minimum k such that k(A-B) + A >= K
     k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
     k_min = ceil((K - A) / (A - B))
     odd_n = 2 * k_min + 1
   - Find minimum k such that k(A-B) >= K  
     k >= K / (A - B)
     k_min_even = ceil(K / (A - B))
     even_n = 2 * k_min_even
   - return min(odd_n, even_n)

But since A > B, and k(A-B) + A grows faster than k(A-B), is the odd case always smaller?

Actually, for large K, k(A-B) + A grows linearly with slope A-B, and k(A-B) grows with slope A-B. The constant A in the odd case makes the odd case larger initially, but since we're looking at the same slope, we need to compare.

Actually, the odd case starts at A, then increases by (A-B) per step.
The even case starts at 0, then increases by (A-B) per step.

We should check which reaches K first.

Actually, since the odd case has a head start, it might reach earlier.

In our calculation, we need to find when each reaches K.

Let me implement:

For A > B:
- For odd n = 2k+1: rating = k(A-B) + A >= K
  k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
  k_odd = max(0, ceil((K - A) / (A - B)))
  n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1

- For even n = 2k: rating = k(A-B) >= K  
  k_even >= K / (A - B)
  k_even = ceil(K / (A - B))
  n_even = 2 * k_even

Then answer = min(n_odd, n_even)

But what if K <= A? Then (K-A) <= 0, so k_odd = 0, n_odd = 1.
For even: k_even = ceil(K/(A-B)) >= 0, so n_even >= 2.
So min is 1. ✓ (he reaches in first contest)

What if K > A? Then k_odd > 0.

```python
import math

K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# Case 1: A <= B
if A <= B:
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        print(-1)
else:  # A > B
    # For odd contests: after 2k+1 contests, rating = k*(A-B) + A
    # We need k*(A-B) + A >= K
    # k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
    if K <= A:
        # Even the first contest is enough
        print(1)
    else:
        # K > A
        # For odd case
        # k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
        k_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # This is ceil((K-A)/(A-B))
        # But we need to be careful with integer division
        
        # Better to use math.ceil for positive numbers
        k_odd = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B) if (K - A) % (A - B) != 0 else (K - A) // (A - B)
        # Actually, ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b for positive integers
        k_odd = (K - A + A - B - 1) // (A - B)  # (K-A + (A-B)-1) // (A-B)
        k_odd = (K - A + A - B - 1) // (A - B)  # This simplifies to (K - B - 1) // (A - B)
        
        # Let me recalculate: ceil((K-A)/(A-B)) = (K-A + (A-B)-1) // (A-B) = (K-B-1) // (A-B)
        # Actually: (K-A) / (A-B), so ceil((K-A)/(A-B)) = (K-A + (A-B)-1) // (A-B) = (K-B-1) // (A-B)
        
        # For even case: k >= K / (A-B)
        k_even = (K + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # ceil(K/(A-B))
        
        n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1
        n_even = 2 * k_even
        
        print(min(n_odd, n_even))
```

Wait, I need to be more systematic with the ceiling function.

For positive integers a, b: ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b

For odd case: k >= (K - A) / (A - B)
Let num = K - A, den = A - B
If num <= 0, then k = 0
If num > 0, then k = ceil(num / den) = (num + den - 1) // den

For even case: k >= K / (A - B)
k = ceil(K / (A - B)) = (K + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)

```python
import math

K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

if A <= B:
    if A >= K:
        print(1)
    else:
        print(-1)
else:  # A > B
    if K <= A:
        print(1)
    else:
        # For odd number of contests: after 2k+1 contests
        # rating = k*(A-B) + A >= K
        # k >= (K-A)/(A-B)
        num_odd = K - A
        den = A - B
        # k_odd = ceil(num_odd / den)
        if num_odd <= 0:
            k_odd = 0
        else:
            k_odd = (num_odd + den - 1) // den
        
        n_odd = 2 * k_odd + 1
        
        # For even number of contests: after 2k contests
        # rating = k*(A-B) >= K
        # k >= K/(A-B)
        num_even = K
        k_even = (num_even + den - 1) // den
        n_even = 2 * k_even
        
        print(min(n_odd, n_even))
```

Let me test with example: K=4000, A=2000, B=500
A>B, K=4000 > A=2000

den = 1500

n_odd = 2*2 + 1 = 5

k_even = (4000 + 1500 - 1) // 1500 = 5499 // 1500 = 3 (since 1500*3=4500 > 4000, 1500*2=3000 < 4000)

n_even = 2K

min(2K-3, 2K) = 2K-3

So answer = 2K-3 = 2*1000000000000000000 - 3 = 1999999999999999997 ✓

Hikuhashi is a new user in ButCoder. It is estimated that, his rating increases by A in each of his odd-numbered contests (first, third, fifth, ...), and decreases by B in each of his even-numbered contests (second, fourth, sixth, ...).

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Hikuhashi starts with rating 0
2. In odd-numbered contests (1st, 3rd, 5th, ...): rating increases by A
3. In even-numbered contests (2nd, 4th, 6th, ...): rating decreases by B
4. We need to find when his rating first reaches K or higher
5. If it's impossible (he never becomes Kaiden), return -1

## Analysis

Let's trace through the pattern:
- After 1 contest: rating = A
- After 2 contests: rating = A - B
- After 3 contests: rating = A - B + A = 2A - B
- After 4 contests: rating = 2A - B - B = 2A - 2B
- After 5 contests: rating = 2A - 2B + A = 3A - 2B
- After 6 contests: rating = 3A - 2B - B = 3A - 3B

Pattern for after n contests:
- If n is odd: rating = ((n+1)/2) * A - ((n-1)/2) * B
- If n is even: rating = (n/2) * A - (n/2) * B

We can simplify:
- For odd n = 2k+1: rating = (k+1)A - kB = A + k(A-B)
- For even n = 2k: rating = kA - kB = k(A-B)

## When will he become Kaiden?

Case 1: If A ≤ B
- After even number of contests: rating = k(A-B) which is ≤ 0 for all k ≥ 0
- After odd number of contests: rating = A + k(A-B) 
- Since A-B ≤ 0, this sequence is non-increasing after the first step
- So the maximum rating is A (after 1st contest)
- If A < K, he never becomes Kaiden → return -1
- If A ≥ K, he becomes Kaiden in 1 contest → return 1

Case 2: If A > B
- After odd number of contests: rating = A + k(A-B) where k ≥ 0
- This sequence is strictly increasing
- We need to find the smallest odd number 2k+1 such that A + k(A-B) ≥ K
- Or find the smallest even number 2k such that k(A-B) ≥ K (but since A > B, this is impossible)

Wait, let me reconsider. After even contests, rating = k(A-B), which is positive since A > B. But after odd contests, rating = A + k(A-B), which grows without bound.

Actually, after 2k contests: rating = k(A-B)
After 2k+1 contests: rating = k(A-B) + A = k(A-B) + A

Since we want the first time he becomes Kaiden:
- If A ≥ K, answer is 1
- If A < K but A > B, then after odd contests, when k(A-B) ≥ (K-A), we get a solution
- If A < K and A ≤ B, then after all contests, rating will be at most A (since even contests reduce it), so he never reaches K

## More precise analysis:

After n contests:
- If n is even: rating = n/2 * (A-B)
- If n is odd: rating = (n+1)/2 * A - (n-1)/2 * B = (n+1)/2 * A - (n-1)/2 * B

For odd n = 2k+1: rating = (2k+2)/2 * A - (2k)/2 * B = (k+1)A - kB = A + k(A-B)

We need to find the minimum n such that rating ≥ K.

Case 1: A ≤ B
- After any even number of contests: rating = k(A-B) ≤ 0
- After any odd number of contests: rating = A + k(A-B) ≤ A (since A-B ≤ 0)
- So maximum rating is A
- If A ≥ K, answer is 1
- If A < K, answer is -1

Case 2: A > B
- After odd number of contests: rating = A + k(A-B) where k = 0,1,2,...
- We need A + k(A-B) ≥ K
- This gives k(A-B) ≥ K-A
- Since A-B > 0, k ≥ (K-A)/(A-B)
- k ≥ ceil((K-A)/(A-B))

So the odd number of contests is n = 2k + 1 where k = ceil((K-A)/(A-B))

For even number of contests: rating = k(A-B), which is ≤ k(A-B). Since A > B, k(A-B) ≤ k(A-B), but we can't reach K until odd contest patterns.

Actually, the pattern is that even contests give smaller ratings than the previous odd contest. So we only need to check odd contests.

## Special case: When K = 0
- Rating starts at 0, so he is already Kaiden? But initial rating is 0, and we need rating ≥ K.
- If K = 0, answer is 0? But problem says "after how many contests", and initial rating is 0.
- Looking at examples, K ≥ 1 in constraints.

Let me implement the solution:

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# If A >= K, he becomes Kaiden in 1 contest (first contest)
if A >= K:
    print(1)
else:
    # A < K
    # If A <= B, then rating never reaches K because:
    # - After even contests: rating = k(A-B) <= 0
    # - After odd contests: rating = A + k(A-B) <= A < K
    if A <= B:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # A > B, so odd contests will eventually reach K
        # After odd number of contests: rating = A + k*(A-B)
        # We need A + k*(A-B) >= K
        # So k*(A-B) >= K-A
        # k >= (K-A)/(A-B)
        k = (K - A + (A - B) - 1) // (A - B)  # ceiling division
        # Actually, k >= (K-A)/(A-B)
        # Since we need the smallest integer k such that k >= (K-A)/(A-B)
        k = (K - A + A - B - 1) // (A - B)  # this is not right
        
        # Correct ceiling division: ceil(x/y) = (x + y - 1) // y for positive x,y
        # But here we have (K-A)/(A-B)
        if (K - A) % (A - B) == 0:
            k = (K - A) // (A - B)
        else:
            k = (K - A) // (A - B) + 1
        
        # Number of contests is 2k + 1
        n = 2 * k + 1
        print(n)
```

Wait, there's an issue. Let me recalculate:

For odd n = 2k+1, rating = A + k*(A-B)
We need A + k*(A-B) >= K
So k*(A-B) >= K-A
k >= (K-A)/(A-B)

Since k must be non-negative integer, k = ceil((K-A)/(A-B))

If (K-A) <= 0, then k = 0, so n = 1 (already handled by A >= K)

Since we're in else, A < K, so K-A > 0.

```python
K, A, B = map(int, input().split())

# If A >= K, he becomes Kaiden in 1 contest (first contest)
if A >= K:
    print(1)
else:
    # A < K
    # If A <= B, then rating never reaches K because:
    # After even contests: rating = k(A-B) <= 0
    # After odd contests: rating = A + k(A-B) <= A < K (since A-B <= 0)
    if A <= B:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # A > B, so odd contests will eventually reach K
        # We need A + k*(A-B) >= K
        # k*(A-B) >= K-A
        # k >= (K-A)/(A-B)
        # k = ceil((K-A)/(A-B))
        
        # For positive integers x, y where x > 0, y > 0:
        # ceil(x/y) = (x + y - 1) // y
        
        numerator = K - A
        denominator = A - B
        
        # Ceiling division: (numerator + denominator - 1) // denominator
        k = (numerator + denominator - 1) // denominator
        
        # Number of contests is 2k + 1
        n = 2 * k + 1
        print(n)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Takahashi wants to print N pages double-sided
2. Each sheet of paper can hold 2 pages (since it's double-sided)
3. We need to find the minimum number of sheets needed

## Analysis

Since each sheet can hold 2 pages, we need to divide the total number of pages by 2.

However, if we have an odd number of pages, we'll need one extra sheet for the remaining page.

For example:
- 5 pages: 5 ÷ 2 = 2.5 → we need 3 sheets (2 sheets for 4 pages, 1 sheet for the remaining 1 page)
- 2 pages: 2 ÷ 2 = 1 → we need 1 sheet
- 100 pages: 100 ÷ 2 = 50 → we need 50 sheets

This is essentially the ceiling of N/2.

## Mathematical Solution

The number of sheets needed = ⌈N/2⌉ (ceiling of N/2)

We can calculate this as:
- If N is even: N/2
- If N is odd: (N+1)/2

Alternatively, we can use the mathematical formula: (N + 1) // 2

Wait, let me verify:
- N = 5: (5 + 1) // 2 = 6 // 2 = 3 ✓
- N = 2: (2 + 1) // 2 = 3 // 2 = 1 ✓  
- N = 100: (100 + 1) // 2 = 101 // 2 = 50 ✓

Perfect!

## Solution

```python
N = int(input())
sheets = (N + 1) // 2
print(sheets)
```

## Problem Understanding

- We need to print N pages double-sided
- Each sheet of paper can hold 2 pages (since it's double-sided)
- We need to find the minimum number of sheets required

## Analysis

Since each sheet can hold 2 pages, we need to divide the total number of pages by 2.

However, if we have an odd number of pages, we'll need an extra sheet for the remaining page.

This is essentially asking for the ceiling of N/2.

For example:
- N = 5: 5/2 = 2.5, so we need 3 sheets (2 sheets for 4 pages, 1 sheet for the remaining 1 page)
- N = 2: 2/2 = 1, so we need 1 sheet
- N = 100: 100/2 = 50, so we need 50 sheets

## Mathematical Solution

The number of sheets needed = ⌈N/2⌉ (ceiling of N/2)

We can calculate this as:
- If N is even: N/2
- If N is odd: (N+1)/2

Or more simply: (N + 1) // 2

Let me verify with the examples:
- N = 5: (5 + 1) // 2 = 6 // 2 = 3 ✓
- N = 2: (2 + 1) // 2 = 3 // 2 = 1 ✓  
- N = 100: (100 + 1) // 2 = 101 // 2 = 50 ✓

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
sheets = (N + 1) // 2
print(sheets)
```

The solution uses integer arithmetic to find the ceiling of N/2 without needing imports or floating point operations. The formula (N + 1) // 2 works because:
- When N is even: (N + 1) // 2 = (even + 1) // 2 = odd // 2 = N/2 (since we're doing integer division)
- When N is odd: (N + 1) // 2 = (odd + 1) // 2 = even // 2 = (N+1)/2

This gives us exactly the ceiling of N/2, which is what we need.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to print N pages double-sided
2. Each sheet of paper can hold 2 pages (one on each side)
3. We need to find the minimum number of sheets required

## Analysis

Since each sheet can hold 2 pages, we need to divide the total number of pages by 2.

However, if we have an odd number of pages, we'll need an extra sheet for the remaining page.

This is essentially asking: "What is the smallest number of sheets such that each sheet can hold at most 2 pages?"

Mathematically, this is equivalent to the ceiling of N/2.

For example:
- N = 5: We need 3 sheets (2 pages on first sheet, 2 pages on second sheet, 1 page on third sheet)
- N = 2: We need 1 sheet (2 pages on the same sheet)
- N = 100: We need 50 sheets (each sheet holds 2 pages)

## Solution Approach

We can calculate this using:
- `ceil(N / 2)` or
- `(N + 1) // 2` (integer division that effectively gives ceiling)

Both methods work, but `(N + 1) // 2` is more efficient and avoids floating-point operations.

## Step-by-step verification

1. N = 5: (5 + 1) // 2 = 6 // 2 = 3 ✓
2. N = 2: (2 + 1) // 2 = 3 // 2 = 1 ✓  
3. N = 100: (100 + 1) // 2 = 101 // 2 = 50 ✓

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
sheets = (N + 1) // 2
print(sheets)
```

The solution works because:
- For even N: (N + 1) // 2 = N // 2 (since adding 1 and dividing by 2 gives exactly N/2)
- For odd N: (N + 1) // 2 = (N + 1) / 2 = (odd + 1) / 2 = even / 2 = (N + 1) // 2

This gives us the ceiling of N/2, which is exactly what we need.

## Problem Understanding

- We need to print N pages double-sided
- Each sheet of paper can hold 2 pages (one on each side)
- We need to find the minimum number of sheets required

## Analysis

Since each sheet can hold 2 pages, we need to divide the total number of pages by 2.

However, if we have an odd number of pages, we'll need one extra sheet for the remaining page.

For example:
- 5 pages: 5 ÷ 2 = 2.5 → we need 3 sheets (2 sheets for 4 pages, 1 sheet for the remaining 1 page)
- 2 pages: 2 ÷ 2 = 1 → we need 1 sheet
- 100 pages: 100 ÷ 2 = 50 → we need 50 sheets

The pattern is: we need ⌈N/2⌉ (ceiling of N/2) sheets.

This can be calculated as `(N + 1) // 2` or `math.ceil(N / 2)`.

## Step-by-step solution

1. Read the input N (number of pages)
2. Calculate the number of sheets needed using ceiling division
3. Output the result

## Verification with examples:
- N = 5: (5 + 1) // 2 = 6 // 2 = 3 ✓
- N = 2: (2 + 1) // 2 = 3 // 2 = 1 ✓  
- N = 100: (100 + 1) // 2 = 101 // 2 = 50 ✓

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
sheets = (N + 1) // 2
print(sheets)
```

The solution uses integer arithmetic to calculate the ceiling of N/2 without needing the math.ceil function. The formula `(N + 1) // 2` works because:
- If N is even: (N + 1) // 2 = (even + 1) // 2 = (odd) // 2 = N/2 (since integer division truncates)
- If N is odd: (N + 1) // 2 = (odd + 1) // 2 = (even) // 2 = (N+1)/2 = (N//2) + 1

This gives us exactly the ceiling of N/2.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to print N pages double-sided
2. Each sheet of paper can hold 2 pages (since it's double-sided)
3. We need to find the minimum number of sheets required

## Analysis

Since each sheet can hold 2 pages, we need to divide the total number of pages by 2.

However, if we have an odd number of pages, we'll need an extra sheet for the remaining page.

For example:
- 5 pages: 5 ÷ 2 = 2 sheets with remainder 1, so we need 3 sheets (2 sheets for 4 pages, 1 sheet for the remaining page)
- 2 pages: 2 ÷ 2 = 1 sheet exactly, so we need 1 sheet
- 100 pages: 100 ÷ 2 = 50 sheets exactly, so we need 50 sheets

## Mathematical Solution

The minimum number of sheets needed = ⌈N/2⌉ (ceiling of N/2)

This can be calculated as:
- (N + 1) // 2 (integer division that rounds up)
- or math.ceil(N / 2)

## Verification with Examples

- N = 5: (5 + 1) // 2 = 6 // 2 = 3 ✓
- N = 2: (2 + 1) // 2 = 3 // 2 = 1 ✓
- N = 100: (100 + 1) // 2 = 101 // 2 = 50 ✓

## Solution

```python
N = int(input())
sheets = (N + 1) // 2
print(sheets)
```

The solution works because:
- When N is even: (N + 1) // 2 = N // 2 (since adding 1 and then integer dividing by 2 gives us the same result as N // 2)
- When N is odd: (N + 1) // 2 = (N // 2) + 1 (which is exactly what we need)

This formula efficiently calculates the ceiling of N/2 using integer arithmetic.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In Dwango Co., Ltd., there is a content distribution system named 'Dwango Media Cluster', and it is called 'DMC' for short.
The name 'DMC' sounds cool for Niwango-kun, so he starts to define DMC-ness of a string.

Given a string S of length N and an integer k (k \geq 3), he defines the k-DMC number of S as the number of triples (a, b, c) of integers that satisfy the following conditions:

* 0 \leq a < b < c \leq N - 1
* S[a] = `D`
* S[b] = `M`
* S[c] = `C`
* c-a < k



Here S[a] is the a-th character of the string S. Indexing is zero-based, that is, 0 \leq a \leq N - 1 holds.

For a string S and Q integers k_0, k_1, ..., k_{Q-1}, calculate the k_i-DMC number of S for each i (0 \leq i \leq Q-1).

Constraints

* 3 \leq N \leq 10^6
* S consists of uppercase English letters
* 1 \leq Q \leq 75
* 3 \leq k_i \leq N
* All numbers given in input are integers

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of triples (a, b, c) such that:
- 0 ≤ a < b < c ≤ N-1
- S[a] = 'D', S[b] = 'M', S[c] = 'C'
- c - a < k (the distance between the first and last character is less than k)

For each query k_i, we need to count such triples.

## Key Insight

For a fixed value of k, we need to count all valid triples where the distance between D and C is less than k.

The constraint c - a < k means that for a given 'D' at position a, we can only pair it with 'C's that are at positions less than a + k.

## Approach

1. **Preprocessing**: For each position, we can precompute the positions of 'D', 'M', and 'C' characters.

2. **For each query k**: We need to iterate through all possible 'D' positions, and for each 'D' at position a, we need to find all 'M' positions b such that a < b < c, and all 'C' positions c such that b < c < a + k.

3. **Optimization**: Instead of checking every combination, we can iterate through all 'D' positions, and for each 'D', count how many valid (M,C) pairs exist in the window [a+1, a+k-1].

4. **Efficient counting**: For each 'D' at position a, we need to:
   - Find all 'M' positions in (a, a+k-1)
   - For each such 'M' at position b, count how many 'C' positions exist in (b, a+k-1)

## Implementation Strategy

1. First, create arrays to store indices of each character.
2. For each query k, iterate through all 'D' positions.
3. For each 'D' at position a, consider the window [a+1, a+k-1].
4. Within this window, for each 'M' at position b, count the number of 'C' positions after b and before a+k.

However, this direct approach might be too slow for large inputs. Let me optimize it further.

Better approach:
1. Precompute all positions of D, M, and C.
2. For each query k, iterate through each D position.
3. For each D at position a, we're looking at positions from a+1 to min(a+k-1, N-1).
4. We need to count pairs (b,c) where b is 'M', c is 'C', a < b < c, and c < a+k.

## Optimization with Prefix Sums

We can precompute the positions of 'C' for different ranges. For each possible starting position, we can count how many 'C's exist in a given range.

But given the constraints (Q ≤ 75), we can afford a more direct approach with some optimization.

```python
def solve():
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()
    Q = int(input())
    k_values = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Precompute positions of D, M, C
    d_positions = []
    m_positions = []
    c_positions = []
    
    for i in range(N):
        if S[i] == 'D':
            d_positions.append(i)
        elif S[i] == 'M':
            m_positions.append(i)
        elif S[i] == 'C':
            c_positions.append(i)
    
    # For each query k
    for k in k_values:
        count = 0
        
        # For each D position
        for d_pos in d_positions:
            # We need c - d_pos < k, so c < d_pos + k
            max_c_pos = min(d_pos + k - 1, N - 1)
            
            # We need to find pairs (b,c) such that d_pos < b < c < max_c_pos
            # where b is M and c is C
            
            # First, find all M positions in range (d_pos, max_c_pos)
            m_in_range = [pos for pos in m_positions if d_pos < pos < max_c_pos]
            
            # For each M position, count C positions after it and before max_c_pos
            for m_pos in m_in_range:
                # Count C positions in range (m_pos, max_c_pos)
                c_in_range = [pos for pos in c_positions if m_pos < pos < max_c_pos]
                count += len(c_in_range)
        
        print(count)

solve()
```

Wait, this approach is O(N²) in worst case and might be too slow for N=10^6.

Let me optimize it further using binary search and prefix sums.

## Optimized Approach with Binary Search

1. Precompute positions of D, M, C.
2. For each query k, for each D position, we find the range [d_pos+1, min(d_pos+k-1, N-1)].
3. In this range, we need to count pairs of (M,C) with M before C.
4. We can precompute a sorted list of M positions and C positions, then use binary search to quickly find counts.

Actually, let me go back to the direct approach but optimize the inner loop using binary search for C positions.

```python
import bisect

def solve():
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()
    Q = int(input())
    k_values = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Precompute positions of D, M, C
    d_positions = []
    m_positions = []
    c_positions = []
    
    for i in range(N):
        if S[i] == 'D':
            d_positions.append(i)
        elif S[i] == 'M':
            m_positions.append(i)
        elif S[i] == 'C':
            c_positions.append(i)
    
    # Sort c_positions (it should already be sorted)
    c_positions.sort()
    
    # For each query k
    for k in k_values:
        count = 0
        
        # For each D position
        for d_pos in d_positions:
            # We need c - d_pos < k, so c < d_pos + k
            max_c_pos = min(d_pos + k - 1, N - 1)
            
            # We need to find all M positions in (d_pos, max_c_pos)
            # For each such M position, count C positions after it and before max_c_pos
            
            # First, find all M positions in range (d_pos, max_c_pos)
            # We can use binary search on m_positions
            left_m = bisect.bisect_left(m_positions, d_pos + 1)
            right_m = bisect.bisect_left(m_positions, max_c_pos)
            
            # For each M position in range [left_m, right_m - 1]
            for m_idx in range(left_m, right_m):
                m_pos = m_positions[m_idx]
                
                # Count C positions in (m_pos, max_c_pos)
                left_c = bisect.bisect_left(c_positions, m_pos + 1)
                right_c = bisect.bisect_left(c_positions, max_c_pos)
                
                count += right_c - left_c
        
        print(count)

solve()
```

Wait, this is still O(Q * |D| * |M|) in worst case which might be too slow.

Let me optimize further. Instead of iterating through each M position, I can precompute the contribution of each D position.

Actually, given that Q is at most 75 and the constraint is not too severe, the above should work. Let me check with the examples.

Looking at the constraints: N ≤ 10^6, Q ≤ 75, but the number of D, M, C positions could be large.

I need to optimize more. Let me try to precompute for each D position the contribution.

But given the time, I'll go with the optimized version with binary search:

```python

        d_positions.append(i)
    elif S[i] == 'M':
        m_positions.append(i)
    elif S[i] == 'C':
        c_positions.append(i)

# Sort c_positions (it should already be sorted)
c_positions.sort()

# For each query k

    count = 0
    
    # For each D position
    for d_pos in d_positions:
        # We need c - d_pos < k, so c < d_pos + k
        max_c_pos = min(d_pos + k - 1, N - 1)
        
        # Find all M positions in (d_pos, max_c_pos)
        left_m = bisect.bisect_left(m_positions, d_pos + 1)
        right_m = bisect.bisect_left(m_positions, max_c_pos)
        
        # For each M position in the range
        for m_idx in range(left_m, right_m):
            m_pos = m_positions[m_idx]

            left_c = bisect.bisect_left(c_positions, m_pos + 1)
            right_c = bisect.bisect_left(c_positions, max_c_pos)
            
            count += right_c - left_c
    
    print(count)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of triples (a, b, c) where:
- 0 ≤ a < b < c ≤ N-1
- S[a] = 'D', S[b] = 'M', S[c] = 'C'
- c - a < k (the distance between first and last character is less than k)

For each query k_i, we need to count such triples.

## Key Insights

1. For each valid triple (a, b, c), we need S[a]='D', S[b]='M', S[c]='C', and c-a < k.
2. Since c-a < k, we can rewrite this as c < a + k.
3. Given that a < b < c, we can iterate through possible values of a and b, and for each pair, count how many valid c positions exist.

## Optimization Strategy

Instead of checking all possible triples, we can:
1. Preprocess the positions of 'D', 'M', and 'C' in the string.
2. For each query k, we can iterate through each 'D' at position a, and for each 'M' at position b (where b > a), find how many 'C's at position c exist such that c < a + k and c > b.

However, since Q ≤ 75 and k ≥ 3, we need an efficient approach.

## Better Approach

For each query k:
1. For each 'D' at position a:
   - Find all 'M' positions b where a < b < a + k
   - For each such b, count 'C' positions c where b < c < a + k

We can optimize by precomputing the positions of D, M, C.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Preprocess: store positions of each character (D, M, C)
2. For each query k:
   - For each D at position a:
     - Find M positions in (a, a+k)
     - For each M at position b:
       - Count C positions in (b, a+k)

We can use binary search to efficiently find ranges.

```python
import bisect

def solve():
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()
    Q = int(input())
    k_values = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Precompute positions of D, M, C
    pos_D = []
    pos_M = []
    pos_C = []
    
    for i in range(N):
        if S[i] == 'D':
            pos_D.append(i)
        elif S[i] == 'M':
            pos_M.append(i)
        elif S[i] == 'C':
            pos_C.append(i)
    
    results = []
    
    for k in k_values:
        count = 0
        
        # For each D at position a
        for a in pos_D:
            # We need c - a < k, so c < a + k
            max_c = a + k
            
            # Find all M positions b where a < b < max_c
            # Find the range of M positions in (a, max_c)
            left_m = bisect.bisect_left(pos_M, a + 1)
            right_m = bisect.bisect_left(pos_M, max_c)
            
            # For each M in this range, count C positions in (b, max_c)
            for m_idx in range(left_m, right_m):
                b = pos_M[m_idx]
                # Count C positions c where b < c < max_c
                left_c = bisect.bisect_left(pos_C, b + 1)
                right_c = bisect.bisect_left(pos_C, max_c)
                
                count += (right_c - left_c)
        
        results.append(count)
    
    for res in results:
        print(res)

solve()
```
