I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n eggs, each with two prices: A's price and G's price
2. For each egg, a_i + g_i = 1000 (given)
3. We need to assign each egg to either A or G
4. The absolute difference between total payment to A and total payment to G must be ≤ 500
5. We need to find such an assignment or output -1 if impossible

## Key Insight

For each egg i:
- If we give it to A, we pay a_i to A and 0 to G
- If we give it to G, we pay g_i to G and 0 to A

The total payment to A will be the sum of a_i for eggs given to A.
The total payment to G will be the sum of g_i for eggs given to G.

Since a_i + g_i = 1000, we can think of this differently.

Let's say we give egg i to A. The contribution to the difference (S_a - S_g) is a_i - g_i.
But since g_i = 1000 - a_i, we have a_i - g_i = a_i - (1000 - a_i) = 2a_i - 1000.

So the contribution of each egg to (S_a - S_g) is (2a_i - 1000).

If we give egg i to G, the contribution to (S_a - S_g) is 0 - (2a_i - 1000) = -2a_i + 1000.

Alternatively, we can think of it as: giving to A adds (a_i - g_i) to the difference, giving to G adds -(a_i - g_i) to the difference.

The total difference D = Σ (a_i - g_i) for eggs given to A.

We want |D| ≤ 500.

For each egg, (a_i - g_i) = 2a_i - 1000.

## Strategy

1. For each egg, calculate the "bias" = a_i - g_i = 2a_i - 1000
2. If we give an egg to A, we add bias to the total difference
3. If we give an egg to G, we add -bias to the total difference

We need the total difference to be in [-500, 500].

One greedy approach:
- Start by assigning all eggs to A (this gives us a baseline difference)
- Calculate the current difference
- For each egg, if giving it to G reduces the absolute difference (or if giving it to A would make it worse), we can consider switching
- Actually, we can just try to minimize the absolute difference

Simple approach:
- Start with all eggs assigned to A
- Calculate total difference D = sum of (a_i - g_i) for all eggs
- If |D| ≤ 500, we're done
- Otherwise, we need to adjust by switching some eggs from A to G

When we switch an egg from A to G:
- The difference changes by -2(a_i - g_i) = -2(2a_i - 1000) = -4a_i + 2000

Wait, let me recalculate:
- Original contribution when assigned to A: (a_i - g_i)
- When assigned to G: -(a_i - g_i)
- Difference change: -(a_i - g_i) - (a_i - g_i) = -2(a_i - g_i)

So switching egg i from A to G reduces the difference by 2(a_i - g_i).

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the initial difference if all eggs go to A: D = sum over i of (a_i - g_i)
2. If |D| ≤ 500, return "A" for all eggs
3. Otherwise, we need to adjust by switching some eggs. For each egg, we can consider switching it from A to G, which changes the difference by -2(a_i - g_i)
4. We want to reach a difference in [-500, 500]

Actually, let's think differently. We can try to minimize the final difference.

We need to solve: sum of (a_i - g_i) for eggs assigned to A = some value D where |D| ≤ 500.

Since each (a_i - g_i) is a fixed value, we can think of this as a subset sum problem where we want to select a subset of eggs to assign to A such that the sum of (a_i - g_i) for selected eggs is in [-500, 500].

But with n up to 10^6, we need an efficient approach.

Key observation: The target range is only [-500, 500], which is quite small.

We can try this:
- Start with all eggs to G (so initial difference = -sum of (a_i - g_i))
- Or start with all eggs to A (difference = sum of (a_i - g_i))

Actually, let's be more systematic.

For each egg i, if we assign it to A, it contributes (a_i - g_i) to the difference.
If we assign it to G, it contributes -(a_i - g_i) to the difference.

We want the total to be in [-500, 500].

Let me define d_i = a_i - g_i = 2a_i - 1000.

We want sum of d_i * x_i where x_i is 1 if assigned to A, 0 if assigned to G, and the sum should be in [-500, 500].

Since we only need to achieve |sum| ≤ 500, and the range is very small, we can start from one extreme and make small adjustments.

But since the total sum might be large, we might need to make multiple adjustments.

Actually, we can try all possible ranges and use dynamic programming, but n is 10^6 so that won't work.

Alternative insight: The values d_i = 2a_i - 1000 can range from -1000 to 1000 (since a_i from 0 to 1000).

But we only need to achieve a final difference in [-500, 500].

We can try to find any assignment. One simple approach:

Start with all eggs assigned to A. This gives a difference D = sum of d_i.

If |D| ≤ 500, we're done.

Otherwise, we need to adjust. We can switch some eggs from A to G.

When we switch egg i from A to G:
- The difference changes by -2 * d_i

So we need to reach a sum in [-500, 500] from our initial sum of D.

We can greedily make changes: for each egg, if switching it would bring us closer to the target, do it.

But we need to do this efficiently.

Actually, since we only need to stay within 500, and we're making changes of at most twice the maximum |d_i| (which is 1000), we can try a greedy approach.

Wait, let's reconsider. The difference we want is in [-500, 500]. 

We can start with all eggs to G: then difference = -sum of d_i.

We can also note that since d_i = 2a_i - 1000, we have d_i ∈ [-1000, 1000].

But I think the simplest approach that works is:

1. Try assigning all eggs to A first
2. Calculate difference D = sum of (a_i - g_i)
3. If |D| ≤ 500, we're done
4. Otherwise, we need to switch some eggs. Since the range is small, we can just start from the full A assignment and greedily switch eggs that help reduce the absolute difference.

Actually, let's just try to find any assignment that satisfies the constraint. We can iterate through possible adjustments, but that might be inefficient.

Another idea: because the constraint is small, we can try to use a greedy method where we check if we can achieve the target by considering the most significant adjustments.

But I think for this problem, since the constraint is quite loose (only 500), and we can achieve within this range by proper assignment, there might always be a solution.

Let me think of a simple algorithm:

- Start with all eggs assigned to A
- Current difference = sum of (a_i - g_i) over all i
- If |difference| ≤ 500, output "A" for all
- Otherwise, we need to switch some eggs to G to reduce the difference
- For each egg, calculate how much difference it changes when switched from A to G: -2*(a_i - g_i)
- Use a greedy approach: keep switching eggs that bring us closest to zero, until we're within 500 or all eggs are considered

But this might not be optimal. However, since we just need any solution, and the difference range is small, it should work.

Actually, let's implement a simpler approach first - just try to find a solution that minimizes the difference, and since we only need |D| ≤ 500, and given the constraint, it should be achievable.

Wait, is it always achievable? 

Since the sum of all d_i might be large, but we can distribute the eggs arbitrarily, and since d_i can be both positive and negative, we should be able to achieve any value in a certain range.

But the problem says "if impossible" so we need to check that.

Actually, the minimum possible |difference| depends on the values.

Let me implement the greedy approach:

1. For each egg, calculate d_i = a_i - g_i
2. Start with all eggs to A: total_diff = sum(d_i)
3. If |total_diff| <= 500, done
4. Else, we need to switch some eggs to G. When we switch egg i, we reduce total_diff by 2*d_i.
5. We can simulate switching eggs one by one, choosing the one that brings us closest to 0, or just keep switching until we're in range.

But we need to be careful not to overshoot or go out of bounds, but since we're reducing the absolute value, and we're on a line, we should be able to reach within 500.

Actually, since the values are discrete, we might not hit exactly, but we only need to go within 500.

Let me try a different approach:

We can compute the target difference we want. We want to find a subset of eggs to assign to A such that sum of d_i for that subset is in [-500, 500].

This is a subset sum with a target range. Since the values can be large, but the target range is small, we can use a greedy approximation.

But let's do this: start with all to A, and then greedily switch eggs that reduce the absolute difference the most.

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))

# Calculate d_i = a_i - g_i
differences = []
for a, g in eggs:
    diff = a - g  # d_i
    differences.append(diff)

# Start with all eggs to A
assignment = ['A'] * n
total_diff = sum(differences)

# Check if already in range
if abs(total_diff) <= 500:
    print(''.join(assignment))
else:
    # We need to switch some eggs to G to reduce |total_diff|
    # When we switch egg i from A to G, total_diff changes by -2 * differences[i]
    # We want to reduce |total_diff| as much as possible
    
    # Make a list of eggs with their difference values
    # We'll greedily switch eggs that reduce the absolute difference the most
    changed = False
    while abs(total_diff) > 500:
        # Find the egg that when switched, reduces |total_diff| the most
        best_idx = -1
        best_reduction = -1
        
        for i in range(n):
            # Switching egg i changes total_diff by -2 * differences[i]
            new_diff = total_diff - 2 * differences[i]
            reduction = abs(new_diff) - abs(total_diff)
            if reduction > 0:  # We only consider reductions
                if reduction > best_reduction or (reduction == best_reduction and best_idx == -1):
                    best_reduction = reduction
                    best_idx = i
        
        if best_idx == -1:
            # No egg can reduce the difference, so impossible
            print(-1)
            exit()
        
        # Switch egg best_idx from A to G
        assignment[best_idx] = 'G'
        total_diff = total_diff - 2 * differences[best_idx]
        
        # Update changes
        changed = True
    
    # If we made a change and are now within range, output assignment
    print(''.join(assignment))
```

Wait, I see a problem. The reduction calculation might not be correct because I'm not comparing the actual improvement.

Actually, I should just pick the egg that brings us closest to zero, regardless of the direction.

Let me fix:

```python

But this might be inefficient for large n, and also, we might make many iterations.

Actually, we can be smarter. We only need to make the difference smaller. Since we're trying to get to a range of size 1000, and the values are discrete, we should be able to reach it.

But is it guaranteed that we can always reach a solution? 

Consider if all d_i are very large, say 1000. Then sum is 1000*n. We need to reduce it by a lot. 

Actually, I think the problem might guarantee that a solution exists because we have the flexibility to assign eggs arbitrarily.

But let me check with the examples:

Example 1:
- Egg 1: a=1, g=999 → d1 = 1-999 = -998
- Egg 2: a=999, g=1 → d2 = 999-1 = 998
- Sum of d_i = -998 + 998 = 0 → |0| ≤ 500 → we can assign as AG

Example 2:
- All eggs have a=400, g=600 → d_i = 400-600 = -200
- Sum = -200 * 3 = -600 → |600| > 500
- Need to reduce the absolute difference to ≤ 500

If we assign all to A: difference = -600
If we assign one to G: difference = -600 - 2*(-200) = -600 + 400 = -200 → |200| ≤ 500

So AGA works.

In the assignment:
- First egg to A: contributes -200
- Second to G: contributes +400 (since switching from A to G: -2*d_i = -2*(-200) = +400)
- Third to A: contributes -200
- Total = -200 + 400 - 200 = 0

Wait, the difference when egg i is to A is d_i, when to G is -d_i.

So if egg i is to G, it contributes -d_i to the total difference.

Total difference = sum of d_i for eggs to A + sum of (-d_i) for eggs to G.

If we give k eggs to G, total difference = sum of d_i for eggs to A - sum of d_i for eggs to G.

But it's simpler: each egg contributes d_i if to A, -d_i if to G.

For example 2: each d_i = -200.

If we give one egg to G, then that egg contributes -(-200) = +200, the other two contribute -200 each.

Total = 200 - 200 - 200 = -200 → |200| ≤ 500.

So it works.

My algorithm should work.

But it might be slow in worst case. However, the difference reduces by at least some amount each time, and since it starts from some value and we reduce it by at least the smallest |2*d_i|, it should terminate.

In worst case, if all d_i are very small, we might need many iterations, but still the absolute difference will eventually be reduced.

Actually, the absolute difference can only be reduced by at least the minimum of |2*d_i| over all i. If all d_i are 0, we're already in range.

If not, we have at least one egg with |d_i| > 0, so we can reduce.

The reduction is at least min(|2*d_i|) for all i, which is positive.

So the number of iterations is at most the initial absolute difference divided by min_increase.

Since the initial difference can be large (up to 10^6 * 1000 = 10^9), and min_increase could be small, it might be too many iterations.

We need a better approach.

Alternative: since we only need |total_diff| ≤ 500, and we can achieve any difference by properly choosing a subset, and since the values can be small, we might be able to make a greedy choice.

But I think the problem is designed such that a simple assignment works, or we can use a different insight.

Actually, we can just try to assign all eggs to A, and if the difference is too large, we can assign some eggs to G to make the difference smaller.

But we can also consider: what if we assign eggs to A only if a_i is small enough?

Another idea: since a_i + g_i = 1000, if a_i < 500, then g_i > 500. If we give to A, we pay less than 500, if give to G, we pay more than 500.

But I think my original algorithm is correct, and in practice with the constraints, it might be acceptable.

But worst case might be too slow.

We can optimize by noting that we only need to make the difference small. We can use a different approach:

- Start with all eggs to A
- Current difference = D
- We need to reduce |D| to ≤ 500
- The amount we need to adjust is |D| - 500 (in absolute terms)

But we can only adjust by multiples of 2*d_i.

This is like a coin problem where we want to reach a certain value.

But since we only care about being within 500, and the values can be large, we can just make the greedy changes.

I think for the given constraints and the fact that we only need a solution, and since the answer range is small, the greedy approach should work in practice.

Let me implement it with a small optimization: break if we already have the solution.

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []

    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))

        min_abs_after = float('inf')

This should work. Let me test with example 2:
- All differences = -200
- Initial sum = -600, |600| > 500
- For each egg, switching gives new_diff = -600 - 2*(-200) = -600 + 400 = -200
- So all choices give new_diff = -200, which has abs 200 ≤ 500
- So we switch one egg, and output the assignment.

In the assignment: first egg to A, second to G, third to A → "AGA"

Yes, matches expected output.

For example 1:
- differences: [-998, 998]
- sum = 0, which is ≤ 500, so output "AG"

Wait, no: first egg (a=1, g=999): d = 1-999 = -998
Second egg (a=999, g=1): d = 999-1 = 998
Sum = -998 + 998 = 0

So output "AG" which is correct.

But in "AG", egg 1 goes to A, egg 2 goes to G.
Contribution: egg 1: -998, egg 2: -998 (since to G, contributes -998)
Total = -998 -998 = -1996? 

I think I made a mistake in the contribution.

Let's recalculate:

For egg i:
- If to A: contributes (a_i - g_i) to total difference
- If to G: contributes (g_i - a_i) = - (a_i - g_i) to total difference

So if to G, contributes -d_i.

In example 1:
- Egg 1 to A: contributes -998
- Egg 2 to G: contributes -998
- Total difference = -998 + (-998) = -1996

This is wrong.

I think I messed up the definition.

Let me redefine:

Let D = total payment to A - total payment to G.

If we give egg i to A: we pay a_i to A, 0 to G, so this contributes a_i to D.
If we give egg i to G: we pay g_i to G, 0 to A, so this contributes -g_i to D.

Since g_i = 1000 - a_i, -g_i = -1000 + a_i.

So:
- To A: contribution a_i
- To G: contribution a_i - 1000

The difference D = sum of (a_i if to A) + (a_i - 1000) if to G.

D = sum of (a_i if to A) + (a_i - 1000) if to G

= sum of a_i for eggs to A + sum of (a_i - 1000) for eggs to G

= sum of a_i over all eggs - 1000 * (number of eggs to G)

Similarly, we can write D = sum of (a_i - g_i) for eggs to A + something.

For egg i:
- If to A: contribution a_i - g_i = a_i - (1000 - a_i) = 2a_i - 1000
- If to G: contribution (a_i - 1000) - (a_i - g_i) = (a_i - 1000) - (2a_i - 1000) = -a_i

This is messy.

Let's go back to the direct definition:

D = sum of a_i for eggs to A - sum of g_i for eggs to G

Since sum of a_i + g_i = 1000 for each egg, sum of g_i = 1000*n - sum of a_i

So D = sum of a_i for eggs to A - (1000*n - sum of a_i for eggs to G)

= sum of a_i for eggs to A - 1000*n + sum of a_i for eggs to G

= (sum of a_i for all eggs) - 1000*n

This is constant! 

Wait, that can't be right.

Let S_a = sum of a_i for eggs to A
S_g = sum of g_i for eggs to G

But for each egg, if to A, we have a_i in S_a, g_i in S_g? No.

Actually, for each egg, it is to exactly one child.

If egg i to A: S_a += a_i, S_g += 0
If egg i to G: S_a += 0, S_g += g_i

So S_a + S_g = sum of a_i + g_i = 1000*n

And D = S_a - S_g = S_a - (1000*n - S_a) = 2*S_a - 1000*n

So we want |2*S_a - 1000*n| ≤ 500

Which means |S_a - 500*n| ≤ 250

So we need the total payment to A to be within [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250]

For each egg, if we give it to A, we pay a_i, if to G, we pay g_i.

We need to find a subset of eggs to give to A such that the sum of a_i for those eggs is in [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250].

This is a subset sum problem with a small range.

The range of S_a is [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250], which is only 500 values.

We can try to find any such subset.

We can start with all eggs to A: S_a = sum of all a_i
Then we can switch eggs from A to G to reduce S_a.

When we switch an egg from A to G:
- We remove a_i from S_a and add g_i to S_g
- S_a changes by -a_i
- The new S_a = old S_a - a_i

We want S_a to be in [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250]

So we can start with S_a0 = sum of a_i over all i
And then iteratively switch eggs that will bring us closer to 500*n.

The amount we need to change is |S_a0 - 500*n|.

We can greedily switch eggs that have the largest a_i (since removing large a_i changes S_a by -a_i, which reduces it if a_i > 0).

If S_a0 > 500*n + 250, we need to reduce it.
If S_a0 < 500*n - 250, we need to increase it (by switching to A from G, or by not switching).

Actually, if we have to increase S_a, we can switch an egg from G to A, which increases S_a by a_i.

Let me implement:

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))

# We need S_a in [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250]
# S_a = sum of a_i for eggs assigned to A

# Start with all to A
S_a = sum(a for a, g in eggs)
target_min = 500 * n - 250
target_max = 500 * n + 250

# If already in range
if target_min <= S_a <= target_max:
    # All to A
    print(''.join(['A'] * n))
else:
    # We need to adjust
    assignment = ['A'] * n
    
    # If S_a > target_max, we need to reduce it by switching some eggs from A to G
    # When we switch egg i from A to G, S_a decreases by a_i
    # We want to reduce until S_a <= target_max
    
    # If S_a < target_min, we need to increase it by switching from G to A
    # But we start with all to A, so we can't switch to A from G yet.
    
    # We need to first consider if we can achieve the range by switching from A to G or from G to A.
    
    # Since we start with all to A, we can only reduce S_a by switching to G.
    # If S_a > target_max, we need to reduce.
    
    # If S_a < target_min, we need to increase S_a, which means we need to assign some eggs to A that we haven't assigned.
    # But we have to start from somewhere.
    
    # Actually, we can do a two-step process: first assign all to A, then adjust.
    # But if we need to increase S_a, we can't because we start from all to A.
    
    # Alternative: start with no eggs to A (all to G), then S_a = 0
    # Then we can assign some eggs to A, increasing S_a by a_i.
    
    # So maybe we should start from all to G and then assign some to A.
    
    # Let's do that.
    
    # Start with all to G: S_a = 0
    S_a = 0
    assignment = ['G'] * n
    
    # We need to assign some eggs to A such that S_a in [target_min, target_max]
    # When we assign egg i to A, S_a increases by a_i
    
    # We want to reach a value in the range.
    
    # Greedy: add eggs to A in order of a_i descending
    # But we need to reach a value in [target_min, target_max]
    
    # Sort eggs by a_i descending
    sorted_eggs = sorted(eggs, key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)
    
    current_S_a = 0
    # Add eggs in descending order of a_i until we're in range or exceed
    # But we might go over, so we need to stop when we're in range.
    
    # We can add eggs one by one in descending order of a_i
    # When we add an egg, current_S_a += a_i
    # We stop when current_S_a >= target_min and current_S_a <= target_max
    
    # But we might need to add some and then stop.
    
    # Actually, since the range is small, we can just add the eggs with largest a_i until we're in range.
    # But we might overshoot.
    
    # Better: we can add eggs until we reach the range.
    
    # We can also try to find any subset.
    
    # Since the range is small, we can just keep adding large a_i eggs until we reach the target or go beyond.
    # But we might go beyond.
    
    # We can use a greedy approach: add eggs with largest a_i until we're in range.
    
    # However, we might need to skip some.
    
    # But since we want any solution, and the range is small, we can do:
    
    current_S_a = 0
    selected = [False] * n
    
    # Sort by a_i descending
    sorted_indices = sorted(range(n), key=lambda i: eggs[i][0], reverse=True)
    
    for idx in sorted_indices:
        a_val = eggs[idx][0]
        if current_S_a + a_val <= target_max and current_S_a + a_val >= target_min:
            # We can add this egg
            current_S_a += a_val
            assignment[idx] = 'A'
        else:
            # Check if adding would help, but we might skip
            # If current_S_a + a_val > target_max, we skip
            # If current_S_a + a_val < target_min, we might want to add, but it's below
            # Since we sorted descending, we add if it fits
            pass
    
    # After this, we might not be in range
    
    # Alternatively, we can go with the first method but start from all to A and reduce when necessary.
    
    # Let's go back to the first approach but with the correct variable.
    
    # We need S_a in [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250]
    # Start with all to A: S_a = sum(a_i)
    S_a = sum(a for a, g in eggs)
    
    assignment = ['A'] * n
    
    # If not in range, we need to reduce S_a by switching some to G
    # When switching to G, S_a -= a_i
    # We want to reduce S_a until it's in [target_min, target_max]
    
    while S_a > target_max:
        # Find the egg with the largest a_i (to reduce S_a by the most)
        best_idx = -1
        best_a = -1
        for i in range(n):
            if assignment[i] == 'A':  # currently to A
                if eggs[i][0] > best_a:
                    best_a = eggs[i][0]
                    best_idx = i
        if best_idx == -1:
            # No egg to A to switch
            print(-1)
            exit()
        # Switch best_idx from A to G
        assignment[best_idx] = 'G'
        S_a -= eggs[best_idx][0]
    
    # Now S_a <= target_max, but it might be < target_min
    while S_a < target_min:
        # We need to increase S_a, so we need to switch from G to A
        # But we have eggs that are currently to G
        # Find the egg with the largest a_i among those to G
        best_idx = -1
        best_a = -1
        for i in range(n):
            if assignment[i] == 'G':
                if eggs[i][0] > best_a:
                    best_a = eggs[i][0]
                    best_idx = i
        if best_idx == -1:
            # No egg to G to switch to A
            print(-1)
            exit()
        # Switch best_idx from G to A
        assignment[best_idx] = 'A'
        S_a += eggs[best_idx][0]
    
    # Now we should be in range
    if target_min <= S_a <= target_max:
        print(''.join(assignment))
    else:
        print(-1)

This is more complex but should work.

However, in the worst case, we might have many iterations, but each iteration changes S_a by at least the minimum a_i, and a_i >= 0, so it should terminate.

But if a_i = 0, then switching doesn't change S_a.

So we need to handle that.

Let me fix the algorithm and simplify.

I think the intended solution is to realize that we need S_a in [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250].

We can start with all to A: S_a = sum of a_i.
If it's in range, done.
Otherwise, we need to adjust by switching from A to G or from G to A.

We can do:

1. If S_a > 500*n + 250, we reduce by switching from A to G (using eggs with large a_i)
2. If S_a < 500*n - 250, we increase by switching from G to A (using eggs with large a_i)

But we start with all to A, so for increasing, we need to first have some to G.

So we need to start with a different assignment.

Final algorithm:

- We can start with all to G: S_a = 0
- Then we can assign some to A to increase S_a
- We want S_a in [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250]

We can use a greedy approach to select a subset of eggs to assign to A, in descending order of a_i, until we're in the range.

This might not be optimal, but since we only need any solution, it should work.

```python

    while S_a > target_max:
        # Find the egg with the largest a_i that is currently to A
        best_idx = -1
        best_a = -1
        for i in range(n):
            if assignment[i] == 'A':
                if eggs[i][0] > best_a:
                    best_a = eggs[i][0]
                    best_idx = i
        if best_idx == -1:
            print(-1)
            exit()
        # Switch to G
        assignment[best_idx] = 'G'
        S_a -= eggs[best_idx][0]
    
    # Now S_a <= target_max, but might be < target_min
    while S_a < target_min:
        # Find the egg with the largest a_i that is currently to G
        best_idx = -1
        best_a = -1
        for i in range(n):
            if assignment[i] == 'G':
                if eggs[i][0] > best_a:
                    best_a = eggs[i][0]
                    best_idx = i
        if best_idx == -1:
            print(-1)
            exit()
        # Switch to A
        assignment[best_idx] = 'A'
        S_a += eggs[best_idx][0]
    
    if target_min <= S_a <= target_max:
        print(''.join(assignment))
    else:
        print(-1)
```

This should work. It handles the in and out of range by switching eggs in order of large a_i.

- We can switch from G to A only if we have eggs to G, but we start with all to A.

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))

# We need |2*S_a - 1000*n| <= 500, which means |S_a - 500*n| <= 250

target_max = 500 * n + 250

# Start with all to A

assignment = ['A'] * n

# If in range, output

    print(''.join(assignment))
else:
    # Try to reduce S_a by switching from A to G
    # or increase by switching from G to A
    # But we start with all to A, so we can only reduce.
    # If we need to increase, we must have some to G.
    # So we try to start with all to G and then assign to A.
    
    # Start with all to G
    S_a = 0
    assignment = ['G'] * n

            S_a += a_val
            assignment[idx] = 'A'

        # If it would go below target_min, skip, but since we are in order, and we only add if in range, it should be safe.
    
    if target_min <= S_a <= target_max:
        print(''.join(assignment))
    else:
        print(-1)

Given the time, I'll output a solution that I believe is correct based on the theory. 

The correct solution is to assign each egg to A or G such that the total payment to A is in [500*n - 250, 500*n + 250].

We can achieve this by a greedy assignment in sorted order. 

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))

target_min = 500 * n - 250
target_max = 500 * n + 250

# We will assign eggs to A if a_i is large, to G if small, in a way to balance.

# Start with all to G, then add to A in descending order of a_i
S_a = 0
assignment = ['G'] * n

# Create list of (a, g, index)
indexed_eggs = [(a, g, i) for i, (a, g) in enumerate(eggs)]
indexed_eggs.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

for a_val, g_val, idx in indexed_eggs:
    if S_a + a_val <= target_max:
        # We can add this egg to A
        S_a += a_val
        assignment[idx] = 'A'

# Check if in range
if target_min <= S_a <= target_max:
    print(''.join(assignment))
else:
    print(-1)
```

Correct theory: |S_a - S_g| ≤ 500.

We can solve by: for each egg, if we give to A, it contributes a_i to S_a, 0 to S_g
 if to G, it contributes 0 to S_a, g_i to S_g.

So we want | (sum of a_i for i to A) - (sum of g_i for i to G) | ≤ 500.

Since for each i, a_i + g_i = 1000, we can write: for each i, if to A: contributes (a_i - 0) to the difference
 if to G: contributes (0 - g_i) = -g_i to the difference.

But a_i - g_i = 2a_i - 1000.

 So the difference D = sum over i of ( a_i if to A, -g_i if to G )

 = sum over i of ( a_i - g_i ) for to A, and for to G, it is -g_i = - (1000 - a_i) = a_i - 1000

 for to A: a_i
 for to G: -g_i = -1000 + a_i

 so for each egg, if to A: contributes a_i to D
 if to G: contributes a_i - 1000 to D

 so D = sum of (a_i for to A) + (a_i - 1000) for to G = sum of a_i over all i - 1000 * (number of to G)

 = sum of a_i - 1000 * (number of to G)

 and we want |D| <= 500.

Let S = sum of a_i over all i
 k = number of eggs to G
 then D = S - 1000*k
 and we want |S - 1000*k| <= 500

 so S - 500 <= 1000*k <= S + 500

 so k in [ (S-500)/1000, (S+500)/1000 ]

 since k must be an integer, we need that there exists an integer k in that interval.

 then we can assign k eggs to G, and the rest to A.

 for each egg, we can choose to assign to G or A.

 to minimize the difference, we can assign to A if it doesn't make the difference large, but we only need any solution.

 so we can: for each egg, if a_i is large, assign to A, if small, to G, to try to balance.

 but to satisfy the condition, we need to choose k such that k is in [ (S-500)/1000, (S+500)/1000 ]

 then we can assign any k eggs to G, and the rest to A, and it will satisfy the condition.

 if no such k exists, then output -1.

 if exists, then choose any k in the range, and assign any k of the eggs to G, the rest to A.

 in the examples:

 example 1: n=2, eggs: (1,999), (999,1)
 S = 1 + 999 = 1000
 k in [ (1000-500)/1000, (1000+500)/1000 ] = [0.5, 1.5] so k=1
 so assign 1 egg to G, one to A.

 for example, assign first to A, second to G: then to G: only second, so k=1, D = 1000 - 1000*1 = 0, |0|<=500, good.

 example 2: n=3, eggs: (400,600) for each
 S = 400*3 = 1200
 k in [ (1200-500)/1000, (1200+500)/1000 ] = [0.7, 1.7] so k=1
 so assign 1 egg to G, two to A.

 then D = 1200 - 1000*1 = 200, |200|<=500, good.

 so any such assignment works.

 algorithm:
- compute S = sum of a_i over all i
- compute k must satisfy (S-500) / 1000 <= k <= (S+500) / 1000
- if there is an integer k in that interval, then output any assignment with k eggs to G, the rest to A
- else, output -1

 to output, we can assign the first k to G, the rest to A, or any.

 in example 1, with k=1, we can output "AG" or "GA"

 in example 2, with k=1, we can output "AGA" or any with one G.

 so the solution is:

```python
n = int(input())
a_list = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    a_list.append(a)

S = sum(a_list)

# k must satisfy (S-500) / 1000 <= k <= (S+500) / 1000
min_k = (S - 500) / 1000
max_k = (S + 500) / 1000

# find integer k in [min_k, max_k]
k = None
for candidate in range(int(min_k), int(max_k) + 1):
    if min_k <= candidate <= max_k:
        k = candidate
        break

if k is None:
    print(-1)
else:
    # output a string with k 'G' and (n-k) 'A'
    # we can output first k as 'G', rest as 'A'
    result = ['A'] * n
    for i in range(k):
        result[i] = 'G'
    print(''.join(result))
```

 any solution is acceptable.

 output "G" for first, "A" for the rest -> "GAA"

n = int(input())
a_list = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    a_list.append(a)

S = sum(a_list)

min_k = (S - 500) / 1000
max_k = (S + 500) / 1000

# Check if there exists an integer k in [min_k, max_k] and in [0, n]
k = None
for candidate in range(0, n+1):
    if min_k <= candidate <= max_k:
        k = candidate
        break

if k is None:
    print(-1)
else:
    result = ['A'] * n
    for i in range(k):
        result[i] = 'G'
    print(''.join(result))

The Bitlandians are quite weird people. They have very peculiar customs.

Uncle J. wants to distribute the eggs between the children so as to give each egg to exactly one child. Also, Uncle J. wants the total money paid to A. to be different from the total money paid to G. by no more than 500.

Help Uncle J. Find the required distribution of eggs or otherwise say that distributing the eggs in the required manner is impossible.


-----Input-----

The first line contains integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^6) — the number of eggs.

Next n lines contain two integers a_{i} and g_{i} each (0 ≤ a_{i}, g_{i} ≤ 1000; a_{i} + g_{i} = 1000): a_{i} is the price said by A. for the i-th egg and g_{i} is the price said by G. for the i-th egg.


-----Output-----

If it is impossible to assign the painting, print "-1" (without quotes).

Otherwise print a string, consisting of n letters "G" and "A". The i-th letter of this string should represent the child who will get the i-th egg in the required distribution. Letter "A" represents A. and letter "G" represents G. If we denote the money Uncle J. must pay A. for the painting as S_{a}, and the money Uncle J. must pay G. for the painting as S_{g}, then this inequality must hold: |S_{a}  -  S_{g}|  ≤  500. 

If there are several solutions, you are allowed to print any of them.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` eggs, each with two prices: `a_i` for child A and `g_i` for child G
2. For each egg, `a_i + g_i = 1000`
3. We need to assign each egg to either A or G such that the absolute difference between total payments to A and G is at most 500
4. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

For each egg, if we assign it to A, we pay `a_i` and G gets nothing from this egg. If we assign it to G, we pay `g_i` and A gets nothing from this egg.

The difference in total payments between A and G will be:
- If egg i goes to A: contributes `a_i - g_i` to difference (since G doesn't get anything from this egg)
- If egg i goes to G: contributes `g_i - a_i` to difference (since A doesn't get anything from this egg)

Actually, let me think differently. If A gets total `S_a` and G gets `S_g`, then:
- `S_a - S_g = sum of (a_i - g_i)` for all eggs assigned to A minus sum of `(a_i - g_i)` for all eggs assigned to G.

But since `a_i + g_i = 1000`, we have `g_i = 1000 - a_i`, so `a_i - g_i = a_i - (1000 - a_i) = 2a_i - 1000`.

So if egg i goes to A, it contributes `(2a_i - 1000)` to the difference `(S_a - S_g)`.
If egg i goes to G, it contributes `-(2a_i - 1000)` to the difference.

Therefore, the total difference is:
`sum over all eggs of [if assigned to A: (2a_i - 1000), if assigned to G: -(2a_i - 1000)]`

We want `|S_a - S_g| ≤ 500`.

## Strategy

The key insight is that we can think of this as a weighted sum. For each egg, if we assign it to A, we add `(2a_i - 1000)` to the difference. If we assign to G, we subtract `(2a_i - 1000)`.

We want the final difference to be between -500 and 500.

Let me define `diff_i = 2a_i - 1000`. Then each egg contributes `diff_i` if assigned to A, or `-diff_i` if assigned to G.

We want the sum to be in range `[-500, 500]`.

Since we can choose freely, we can initially assign all eggs to A. Then the total difference is `sum(diff_i)`.

If this sum is within [-500, 500], we're done. Otherwise, we need to reassign some eggs from A to G to reduce the difference.

When we reassign an egg from A to G, we change the difference by `-2 * diff_i` (we remove `diff_i` and add `-diff_i`, so net change is `-2 * diff_i`).

We need to find a subset of eggs to reassign such that the final difference is in range [-500, 500].

Actually, let's think simpler. We can compute the difference if we assign all to A: `total_diff = sum(2a_i - 1000)`.

If `|total_diff| ≤ 500`, we're done.

If `total_diff > 500`, we need to reduce it by at least `(total_diff - 500)`. We can do this by reassigning eggs from A to G. When we reassign egg i from A to G, we reduce the difference by `2 * (2a_i - 1000)`.

- When assigned to G: `-(2a_i - 1000)`

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the initial difference if all eggs go to A: `total_diff = sum(2a_i - 1000)`
2. If `|total_diff| ≤ 500`, output all A's
3. Otherwise, we need to reassign some eggs to reduce the difference

The target range is `[-500, 500]`.

We can start by assigning all to A and then reassign some to G to reduce the difference (if it's too large).

For eggs with `d_i > 0`, reassigning from A to G reduces the difference by `2 * d_i`.

For eggs with `d_i < 0`, reassigning from A to G increases the difference (bad).

So we only consider eggs with `d_i > 0`.

We need to reduce the difference by `max(0, total_diff - 500)` if total_diff > 500, or increase by `max(0, -total_diff - 500)` if total_diff < -500.

But since we can only reduce by reassigning positive d_i eggs, and increasing by reassigning negative d_i eggs, we need to check if we can achieve the target.

Actually, let me think again. If we assign eggs to G, the difference `(S_a - S_g)` changes.

When we reassign egg i from A to G:
- The difference decreases by `2 * (2a_i - 1000)` if `(2a_i - 1000) > 0`
- The difference increases by `2 * (2a_i - 1000)` if `(2a_i - 1000) < 0`

So if `total_diff > 500`, we need to reduce it by at least `total_diff - 500`.
We can do this by reassigning eggs with `d_i > 0`.

If `total_diff < -500`, we need to increase it by at least `500 - total_diff`.
We can do this by reassigning eggs with `d_i < 0`.

But in both cases, we're only changing the sum of differences, so we can find a subset of eggs to reassign.

For simplicity, let's try a greedy approach:

1. Calculate initial difference with all to A: `diff = sum(2a_i - 1000)`
2. If `|diff| <= 500`, return all A's
3. If `diff > 500`, we need to reduce by at least `diff - 500`. Reassign eggs with `a_i > 500` (which gives positive d_i) in order of decreasing `2a_i - 1000`.
4. If `diff < -500`, we need to increase by at least `500 - diff`. Reassign eggs with `a_i < 500` (which gives negative d_i) in order of decreasing `|2a_i - 1000|`.

Actually, if `d_i < 0`, reassigning from A to G increases the difference by `2 * |d_i|`.

Wait, if `d_i < 0`, then `2a_i - 1000 < 0`, so `a_i < 500`.
When reassigning from A to G, we change from `d_i` to `-d_i`, so the difference changes by `-d_i - d_i = -2d_i > 0` (since d_i < 0).

So it increases the difference by `-2d_i = 2 * (1000 - 2a_i)`.

So in both cases, reassigning eggs with `a_i > 500` (positive d_i) reduces the difference, and reassigning eggs with `a_i < 500` (negative d_i) increases it.

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))

# Calculate initial difference if all eggs go to A
# Each egg i: if given to A, difference contribution is (2a_i - 1000)
# If given to G, difference contribution is -(2a_i - 1000)
total_diff = 0
for a, g in eggs:
    total_diff += 2 * a - 1000

# If within range, we're done
if abs(total_diff) <= 500:
    print('A' * n)
else:
    # We need to reassign some eggs to fix the difference
    # We'll create a list of eggs with their "effect" on difference
    # For reassignment, when we move egg from A to G:
    # - From diff contribution of (2a_i - 1000) to - (2a_i - 1000)
    # - Change in total difference: -2 * (2a_i - 1000)
    
    # We need to move enough eggs to make |difference| <= 500
    # Current difference is total_diff
    target = 500
    
    # If current difference is positive, we need to reduce it
    # If negative, we need to increase it
    
    result = ['A'] * n
    
    # Build list of eggs that can help us adjust the difference
    # Eggs where a_i > 500 will reduce difference when moved to G
    # Eggs where a_i < 500 will increase difference when moved to G
    positive_eggs = []  # will reduce difference when moved to G
    negative_eggs = []  # will increase difference when moved to G
    
    for i, (a, g) in enumerate(eggs):
        diff_contrib = 2 * a - 1000
        if diff_contrib > 0:
            positive_eggs.append((diff_contrib, i))
        elif diff_contrib < 0:
            negative_eggs.append((-diff_contrib, i))  # negative of diff_contrib for increase
    
    # Sort by the change they provide (largest first)
    positive_eggs.sort(reverse=True, key=lambda x: x[0])
    negative_eggs.sort(reverse=True, key=lambda x: x[0])
    
    current_diff = total_diff
    need_to_reduce = 0
    need_to_increase = 0
    
    # If current_diff > 500, we need to reduce
    if current_diff > 500:
        need_to_reduce = current_diff - 500
        # Use positive_eggs (which reduce difference)
        adjustment = 0
        for change, idx in positive_eggs:
            if adjustment >= need_to_reduce:
                break
            adjustment += change * 2  # when we move to G, we reduce by 2*change
            result[idx] = 'G'
        # If we still need reduction, it's impossible
        if adjustment < need_to_reduce:
            print(-1)
        else:
            print(''.join(result))
    
    # If current_diff < -500, we need to increase
    elif current_diff < -500:
        need_to_increase = 500 - current_diff  # we need to increase by this much
        adjustment = 0
        for change, idx in negative_eggs:
            if adjustment >= need_to_increase:
                break
            adjustment += change * 2  # when we move to G, we increase by 2*change
            result[idx] = 'G'
        # If we still need increase, it's impossible
        if adjustment < need_to_increase:
            print(-1)
        else:
            print(''.join(result))
    
    # If total_diff is already in range, we already handled it above
    # But we didn't handle the case where total_diff is in range
```

```python

    # We need to reassign some eggs to fix the difference

    # When we move an egg from A to G:
    # - Difference decreases by 2*(2a_i - 1000) if 2a_i - 1000 > 0
    # - Difference increases by 2*(1000 - 2a_i) if 2a_i - 1000 < 0

    helpful_eggs = []
    for i, (a, g) in enumerate(eggs):
        diff_contrib = 2 * a - 1000
        if diff_contrib > 0:
            # Moving this egg to G reduces difference by 2 * diff_contrib
            helpful_eggs.append((2 * diff_contrib, i, 'reduce'))
        elif diff_contrib < 0:
            # Moving this egg to G increases difference by 2 * (-diff_contrib)
            helpful_eggs.append((2 * (-diff_contrib), i, 'increase'))
    
    # Sort by value (largest first) to get the most effective changes
    helpful_eggs.sort(reverse=True)
    
    result = ['A'] * n
    current_diff = total_diff
    
    # We need to achieve |current_diff| <= 500
    if current_diff > 500:
        # Need to reduce by at least (current_diff - 500)
        need_reduction = current_diff - 500
        adjustment = 0
        for change, idx, direction in helpful_eggs:
            if adjustment >= need_reduction:
                break
            adjustment += change
            result[idx] = 'G'
        if adjustment < need_reduction:
            print(-1)
        else:
            print(''.join(result))

            result[idx] = 'G'

Since |G| can range from 0 to n, we need T - 1000 * k to be in [-500, 500] for some k in [0, n].

This means 1000k ∈ [T-500, T+500]

Therefore, there is always such a k in [0, n]?

For example, if T is very large, say T = 1000000, then we need 1000k in [999500, 1000500], so k in [999.5, 1000.5], so k=1000.

But if n < 1000, then k=1000 is not allowed.

Similarly, if T is very small, say T=0, then we need 1000k in [-500, 500], so k=0.

So the condition is: there exists integer k in [0, n] such that |T - 1000k| ≤ 500.

This is equivalent to: there exists k in [0, n] such that k in [ (T-500)/1000, (T+500)/1000 ]

Since the length is 1000, there is at least one multiple of 1000 in any interval of length 1000, but we need it within [0, n].

The number of multiples of 1000 in [0, n] is floor(n/1000) + 1.

We can find the candidate k values:

- The closest multiple of 1000 to T is floor(T/1000) * 1000 or ceil(T/1000) * 1000
- We need to check if any of these is within 500 of T

Actually, the interval [T-500, T+500] will contain a multiple of 1000 if and only if the fractional part of T/1000 is such that it's within 0.5 of an integer.

More precisely, T mod 1000 must be in [0, 500] or in [500, 1000] - actually, the interval [T-500, T+500] contains a multiple of 1000 if and only if the distance from T to the nearest multiple of 1000 is at most 500.

The distance from T to nearest multiple of 1000 is min(|T mod 1000|, 1000 - (T mod 1000)).

We need this distance ≤ 500.

This is always true because min(d, 1000-d) ≤ 500 for any d, since if d ≤ 500, min is d ≤ 500, if d > 500, min is 1000-d < 500.

min(d, 1000-d) is at most 500 for all d.

Therefore, for any T, there exists a multiple of 1000 within 500 of T.

But we also need k to be in [0, n].

k = floor(T/1000) or k = ceil(T/1000), whichever is in [0, n].

So the algorithm is:

1. Calculate T = sum of all a_i
2. Find integer k in [0, n] such that |T - 1000k| ≤ 500
3. This is always possible for some k because the distance to nearest multiple of 1000 is at most 500, and we just need to pick the one in [0, n].

But is it always in [0, n]? The multiple of 1000 closest to T might be negative or greater than n.

For example, if T is very large and n is small.

Let's say T = 1000 * n + 500, then the distance to 1000*n is 500, so k=n works.

If T = 1000 * n + 600, then distance to 1000*n is 600 > 500, distance to 1000*(n+1) is 400, but if n+1 > n, it's not allowed.

So we need to check if there exists k in [0, n] such that |T - 1000k| ≤ 500.

We can iterate k from 0 to n, and see if any satisfies the condition.

Since n can be up to 10^6, and we can iterate over k from 0 to n, that's 10^6 which is acceptable.

Then for each such k, we need to assign exactly k eggs to G, and the rest to A.

To decide which k to choose, we can pick any that satisfies the condition.

Then we need to decide which eggs to assign to G.

Since the difference only depends on the count of eggs assigned to G, not which ones, we can assign any k eggs to G.

So the algorithm:

1. T = sum of all a_i
2. For k in range(0, n+1):
   - if |T - 1000*k| <= 500:
     - we can choose this k
     - assign any k eggs to G, the rest to A
     - output the assignment
3. If no such k exists, output -1

But is it possible that no such k exists? We argued that for any T, there is a multiple of 1000 within 500, but we need it in [0, n].

For example, if T = 1000 * (n+1) + 400, then distance to 1000*(n+1) is 400 ≤ 500, but k = n+1 > n, not allowed.
Distance to 1000*n is 1000 - (T - 1000*n) = 1000 - (1000 + 400 - 1000*n) = 1000 - (400 - 1000*n) = 1000 - 400 + 1000*n = 600 + 1000*n > 500.

So only the multiple of 1000 that is in [0, n] needs to be within 500.

So we must iterate over k in [0, n] and check.

Since the function |T - 1000k| is piecewise linear, we can optimize.

The values of k that are close to T/1000 will have small |T - 1000k|.

So we can start from k0 = T // 1000, and check k0, k0+1, k0-1, etc.

But to be safe, since n <= 10^6, we can iterate from max(0, floor(T/1000) - 1) to min(n, ceil(T/1000) + 1), and check.

But the minimum distance is at most 500, so the closest multiple of 1000 should be within 500 of T, and we only need to check a few candidates.

Actually, since the distance to the nearest multiple of 1000 is at most 500, the nearest multiple of 1000 is within 500, so we only need to check the values around T/1000.

We can do:

k_candidate = T // 1000
# Check k_candidate, k_candidate-1, k_candidate+1, but within [0, n]

But let's be safe and try a few.

Since the condition |T - 1000k| <= 500 is equivalent to k being in [ (T-500)/1000, (T+500)/1000 ], and this interval has length 1000, it contains at least one multiple of 1000, and we only need to check if any multiple in [0, n] is in this interval.

The candidates are floor((T-500)/1000) and ceil((T+500)/1000), but we can check nearby values.

But let's just iterate over k from 0 to n and see if any satisfies. n is 10^6, which is acceptable in Python.

However, worst-case 10^6 iterations is acceptable.

But we can do better.

The values of k that can work are those where k is in [ (T-500)/1000, (T+500)/1000 ] and k in [0, n].

So we can find the range of k.

Let low = (T - 500) / 1000.0
Let high = (T + 500) / 1000.0

Then we need k in [max(0, ceil(low)), min(n, floor(high))]

So we can find the integer range.

But to be safe, I'll do a simple loop from 0 to n.

But for performance, let's find the best candidate.

The best k is floor(T/1000) or ceil(T/1000), whichever is in [0, n].

Let k1 = T // 1000
k2 = (T + 999) // 1000  # ceil(T/1000)

Then check if |T - 1000*k1| <= 500 and k1 in [0, n]
Or if |T - 1000*k2| <= 500 and k2 in [0, n]

But since the distance to nearest multiple of 1000 is at most 500, at least one of k1 or k2 will have distance <= 500.

So we can do:

k_candidates = []
k1 = T // 1000
k2 = (T + 999) // 1000

# k1 and k2 are the floor and ceil of T/1000

if 0 <= k1 <= n and abs(T - 1000 * k1) <= 500:
    k = k1
elif 0 <= k2 <= n and abs(T - 1000 * k2) <= 500:
    k = k2
else:
    # no candidate in [0, n]
    print(-1)
    exit()

Then we need to assign any k eggs to G, and the rest to A.

For example, we can assign the first k eggs to G, and the rest to A.

But we need to ensure the difference is within range, which by construction it is.

Let me verify with example 1:
n=2, eggs: (1,999), (999,1)
T = 1 + 999 = 1000
k candidates: k1 = 1000 // 1000 = 1, |1000 - 1000*1| = 0 <= 500, and 0<=1<=2, so k=1.
Assignment: first egg to G, second to A: "GA"
Or first to A, second to G: "AG"

Check: if "AG": A gets 1, G gets 999 -> difference = 1 - 999 = -998, |diff| = 998 > 500. 
Wait, this is not right.

I think I made a serious mistake.

S_a = sum of a_i for assigned to A
S_g = sum of g_i for assigned to G

For "AG": A gets egg1: a1=1, G gets egg2: g2=1
S_a = 1, S_g = 1, |1-1|=0 <= 500. Good.

For "GA": A gets egg2: a2=999, G gets egg1: g1=999
S_a = 999, S_g = 999, difference 0. Good.

So either is fine.

In my formula, T = sum of all a_i = 1 + 999 = 1000
For k=1, |1000 - 1000*1| = 0 <= 500, and we have one egg to G.

So the condition is correct.

For the assignment, we can assign any k eggs to G.

Example 2: n=3, all (400,600)

Assign one egg to G, two to A.

For example, assign first to G: "GAA"

S_g = 600
|800-600| = 200 <= 500. Good.

The problem asks to output any valid assignment.

So the algorithm is:

1. Read n
2. Read the n pairs (a_i, g_i)
3. T = sum of all a_i
4. Find k in [0, n] such that |T - 1000*k| <= 500
5. If found, assign any k eggs to G, the rest to A (e.g., first k eggs to G)
6. If not found, output -1

To find k efficiently, we can find the candidate k values around T/1000.

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
T = 0
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))
    T += a

# Find k in range [0, n] such that |T - 1000*k| <= 500
# The values of k that are close to T/1000 will work
k_candidates = []

# The nearest multiple of 1000 to T
k1 = T // 1000
k2 = (T + 999) // 1000  # ceil(T/1000)

# Check k1 and k2
for k in [k1, k2]:
    if 0 <= k <= n and abs(T - 1000 * k) <= 500:
        k = k
        break
else:
    # Check also k in [0, n] that might work
    # Since the difference function is linear, the only candidates are near T/1000
    # But to be safe, we can also check if there's any k in [0, n] with |T-1000*k| <= 500
    # We'll do a small range
    k = None
    # We only need to check k around T/1000
    start = max(0, k1 - 1)
    end = min(n, k2 + 1)
    for k_try in range(start, end + 1):
        if abs(T - 1000 * k_try) <= 500:
            k = k_try
            break
    if k is None:
        print(-1)
        exit()

# Assign first k eggs to G, rest to A
result = []
for i in range(n):
    if i < k:
        result.append('G')
    else:
        result.append('A')
print(''.join(result))
```

Let me test with examples.

Example 1: n=2, T=1000
k1 = 1000//1000 = 1, |1000-1000*1|=0<=500, so k=1.
Result: "GA" or "AG" depending on indexing.

In the loop, i from 0 to 1:
i=0: i<1 -> 'G'
i=1: i>=1 -> 'A'
So "GA"

Example 2: n=3, T=1200
k1 = 1200//1000 = 1, |1200-1000|=200<=500, so k=1.
Result: first one to G, rest to A: "GAA"

All good.

Edge case: what if no k works in [0,n]?

For example, if T = 1000*n + 501, then for k=n: |T-1000*n| = 501 > 500
k=n-1: |T-1000*(n-1)| = |1000*n+501 - 1000*n + 1000| = 1501 > 500
And for k=n+1 not allowed.

Is there any k with |T - 1000k| <= 500?

The distance from T to nearest multiple of 1000 is min(|T mod 1000|, 1000 - (T mod 1000))

T mod 1000 = 501, so min(501, 499) = 499 <= 500, so there is a multiple within 500.

The multiple of 1000 closest to T is at distance 499.

If T = 1000*n + 501, then the multiple is 1000*n or 1000*(n+1) or others.

Distance to 1000*n = 501 > 500
Distance to 1000*(n+1) = 1000*(n+1) - (1000*n + 501) = 499 <= 500

So k = n+1 is a candidate, but k must be in [0,n] and n+1 > n, so not allowed.

And there is no other k in [0,n] with distance <=500.

So we output -1.

Therefore, the condition is not always satisfied.

In our candidate search, when we check k1 and k2, we only check if they are in [0,n] and satisfy the distance.

If not, we try a small range around the nearby values.

In the case above, T = 1000*n + 501, so T//1000 = n, (T+999)//1000 = (1000*n + 501 + 999)//1000 = (1000*n + 1500)//1000 = n + 1.5, so (T+999)//1000 = n+1

So k1 = n, distance = 501 > 500, so not valid.
k2 = n+1, which is > n, so not in [0,n].

Then our small range: from max(0, n-1) to min(n, n+1+1) = from n-1 to n.

Check k=n-1: |T - 1000*(n-1)| = |1000*n + 501 - 1000*n + 1000| = 1501 > 500
k=n: |501| = 501 > 500

No valid k.

So output -1.

This is correct.

Therefore, final code:

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
T = 0
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))
    T += a

# Find k in [0, n] such that |T - 1000*k| <= 500
k = None

# Try the candidates around T/1000
k1 = T // 1000
k2 = (T + 999) // 1000  # ceil(T/1000)

# Check these candidates
for k_candidate in [k1, k2]:
    if 0 <= k_candidate <= n and abs(T - 1000 * k_candidate) <= 500:
        k = k_candidate
        break

# If not found, check a small range around the candidates
if k is None:
    start = max(0, k1 - 1)
    end = min(n, k2 + 1)
    for k_candidate in range(start, end + 1):
        if abs(T - 1000 * k_candidate) <= 500:
            k = k_candidate
            break

if k is None:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Assign first k eggs to G, rest to A
    result = []
    for i in range(n):
        if i < k:
            result.append('G')
        else:
            result.append('A')
    print(''.join(result))
```

Let me test with the examples.

Example 1: n=2, T=1+999=1000
k1=1, |1000-1000|=0<=500, k=1
Output: "GA"

Example 2: n=3, T=400+400+400=1200

Note: the assignment of which eggs to G doesn't matter, since the difference only depends on the count of eggs assigned to G (as shown in the derivation).

Therefore, we can assign any k eggs to G.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
T = 0
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    T += a

# Find k in [0, n] such that |T - 1000*k| <= 500
k = None

# Try the floor and ceil of T/1000
k1 = T // 1000
k2 = (T + 999) // 1000  # ceil division

# Check if either works
for candidate in [k1, k2]:
    if 0 <= candidate <= n and abs(T - 1000 * candidate) <= 500:
        k = candidate
        break

# If not found, check a small range around the candidates
if k is None:
    start = max(0, k1 - 1)
    end = min(n, k2 + 1)
    for candidate in range(start, end + 1):
        if abs(T - 1000 * candidate) <= 500:
            k = candidate
            break

if k is None:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Assign first k eggs to G
    result = ['G'] * k + ['A'] * (n - k)
    print(''.join(result))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The Bitlandians are quite weird people. They have very peculiar customs.

As is customary, Uncle J. wants to have n eggs painted for Bitruz (an ancient Bitland festival). He has asked G. and A. to do the work.

The kids are excited because just as is customary, they're going to be paid for the job! 

Overall uncle J. has got n eggs. G. named his price for painting each egg. Similarly, A. named his price for painting each egg. It turns out that for each egg the sum of the money both A. and G. want for the painting equals 1000.

Uncle J. wants to distribute the eggs between the children so as to give each egg to exactly one child. Also, Uncle J. wants the total money paid to A. to be different from the total money paid to G. by no more than 500.

Help Uncle J. Find the required distribution of eggs or otherwise say that distributing the eggs in the required manner is impossible.


-----Input-----

The first line contains integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^6) — the number of eggs.

Next n lines contain two integers a_{i} and g_{i} each (0 ≤ a_{i}, g_{i} ≤ 1000; a_{i} + g_{i} = 1000): a_{i} is the price said by A. for the i-th egg and g_{i} is the price said by G. for the i-th egg.


-----Output-----

If it is impossible to assign the painting, print "-1" (without quotes).

Otherwise print a string, consisting of n letters "G" and "A". The i-th letter of this string should represent the child who will get the i-th egg in the required distribution. Letter "A" represents A. and letter "G" represents G. If we denote the money Uncle J. must pay A. for the painting as S_{a}, and the money Uncle J. must pay G. for the painting as S_{g}, then this inequality must hold: |S_{a}  -  S_{g}|  ≤  500. 

If there are several solutions, you are allowed to print any of them.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
1 999
999 1

Output
AG

Input
3
400 600
400 600
400 600

Output
AGA### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` eggs, each with two prices: A's price and G's price
2. For each egg, a_i + g_i = 1000 (given)
3. We need to assign each egg to either A or G
4. The absolute difference between total payments to A and G must be ≤ 500
5. We need to find such an assignment or return -1 if impossible

## Key Insight

For each egg i, if we give it to A, we pay a_i and get 0 for G.
If we give it to G, we pay g_i and get 0 for A.

The difference between totals is: |sum(a_i for eggs given to A) - sum(g_i for eggs given to G)|

Since a_i + g_i = 1000, we can reframe this:
- If egg i goes to A: contributes (a_i - g_i) to the difference (since g_i is paid to G)
- If egg i goes to G: contributes (g_i - a_i) = -(a_i - g_i) to the difference

Let's define d_i = a_i - g_i. Then:
- If egg i goes to A: contributes +d_i to the difference
- If egg i goes to G: contributes -d_i to the difference

We want |sum of contributions| ≤ 500.

## Strategy

The total difference without any constraints would be the sum of d_i * x_i where x_i is +1 if given to A, -1 if given to G.

But we can think of it differently: we want to minimize the absolute difference.

Since a_i + g_i = 1000, we can write:
- Paying to A: cost = a_i
- Paying to G: cost = g_i = 1000 - a_i

The difference between total costs is |sum of (a_i if given to A) - (g_i if given to G)|

Actually, let me reframe:
Total cost to A: sum(a_i for eggs to A)
Total cost to G: sum(g_i for eggs to G)

Difference = |sum(a_i for eggs to A) - sum(g_i for eggs to G)|

We can write the difference as sum of (a_i - g_i) for eggs where we assign A, minus (g_i - a_i) for eggs where we assign G.

Actually, simpler: if egg i goes to A, it contributes (a_i - g_i) to the difference.
If egg i goes to G, it contributes (g_i - a_i) = -(a_i - g_i) to the difference.

So total difference = sum over all eggs of c_i * s_i where c_i = a_i - g_i, and s_i = +1 if A, -1 if G.

We want |total difference| ≤ 500.

Since we have freedom to assign each egg, we can think of this as trying to balance the sum.

## Approach

A greedy approach might work:
- For each egg, calculate the "advantage" of giving it to A or G
- The "advantage" of giving to A is (a_i - g_i) = (a_i - (1000 - a_i)) = 2*a_i - 1000
- The advantage of giving to G is (g_i - a_i) = -2*a_i + 1000

We want to minimize |sum of advantages|.

Actually, we can use a simple greedy: assign each egg to the one with the lower cost, but we need to ensure the final difference is ≤ 500.

Wait, let me think more systematically.

## Optimal Strategy

Consider the difference in total payments:
- If we give egg i to A: contributes a_i to A's total, so difference = (sum of a_i for A) - (sum of g_i for G)
- If we give egg i to G: contributes g_i to G's total, so difference = (sum of a_i for A) - (sum of g_i for G)

Actually, the difference between A's and G's payments is:
|sum(a_i for A) - sum(g_i for G)|

Since sum(a_i + g_i) = sum(1000) = 1000n, we can express this as:
Let S_A = sum of a_i for eggs given to A
Then S_G = sum of g_i for eggs given to G = total - S_A

The difference |S_A - (total - S_A)| = |2*S_A - total| ≤ 500

So we need 2*S_A - total ∈ [-500, 500], which means S_A ∈ [total/2 - 250, total/2 + 250]

Where total = sum of all a_i (since sum of all a_i + g_i = 1000n, and sum of all a_i = total).

So we need to find a subset of eggs such that sum of a_i for eggs in subset is in [total/2 - 250, total/2 + 250].

This is a subset sum problem with a target range.

Since n ≤ 10^6, we can't do brute force. But we can use a greedy approach.

## Simple Greedy

Idea: for each egg, if giving it to A gives a benefit (a_i > g_i), we might want to give it to A, otherwise to G.

But we need to ensure the final difference is ≤ 500.

Actually, let's consider the difference contribution: if we give egg i to A, it contributes (a_i - g_i) to the difference (A's total minus G's total).

Since a_i + g_i = 1000, a_i - g_i = 2a_i - 1000.

So if a_i > 500, giving to A gives positive contribution.
If a_i < 500, giving to A gives negative contribution.

We want the total contribution to be in [-500, 500].

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the "bias" of each egg: b_i = a_i - g_i = 2*a_i - 1000
2. Initially assign all eggs to G (so total contribution = 0)
3. For each egg, if giving it to A would reduce the absolute difference, give it to A.

Wait, that's not exactly right. If we start with all to G, contribution = 0.
Giving egg i to A: contribution changes by b_i (because A gets a_i, G gets g_i, so difference changes by (a_i - g_i) = b_i).

So we want to assign eggs such that sum of b_i for assigned eggs = total_dif, and |total_dif| ≤ 500.

This is similar to finding a subset with sum in a given range.

But we can use a simpler greedy approach: start with all eggs to G (contribution 0), then for each egg, if giving it to A would keep us within the 500 limit, do it.

However, since we have to make a definite assignment, and the differences are bounded, we can try:

For each egg, if we give it to A, the contribution changes by b_i = a_i - g_i.
We want the final sum to be in [-500, 500].

We can simulate a greedy: start with all to G, then for each egg in some order, if the contribution would stay within bounds, assign to A.

But the order might matter.

Actually, we can do: assign eggs to G by default, then for each egg, if we can assign it to A and still stay within bounds, do it.

But we need to be careful about the order.

A simpler approach: if we can achieve any assignment where the total difference is ≤ 500, we just need to find one.

Since the constraints are symmetric, and the differences are small (only ±500), we can use a greedy that always assigns to the one with smaller cost.

But let me test with examples:

Example 1: n=2, eggs: (1,999), (999,1)
- Egg 1: a=1, g=999 → a is cheaper → give to A
- Egg 2: a=999, g=1 → g is cheaper → give to G
- A's total: 1, G's total: 1 → difference = 0 ≤ 500 ✓

Example 2: n=3, all (400,600)

So "AGA" works.

My simple greedy (always assign to cheaper) gives A for all, which has difference 1200 > 500.

So I need a better approach.

## Better Strategy

Idea: try to balance the assignments.

For each egg, if we give it to A, the difference between A and G changes by (a_i - g_i) = 2*a_i - 1000.

We want the sum of these changes to be in [-500, 500].

We can start with all eggs assigned to G (sum = 0), then try to "shift" some eggs from G to A, which will add the difference (a_i - g_i).

We want the final sum to be in [-500, 500].

Since we can shift each egg independently, we just need to check if there's a subset of eggs that we can assign to A such that the total sum of (a_i - g_i) is in [-500, 500].

This is subset sum in a range. But n is up to 10^6, so we can't do subset sum.

However, the range is small (only ±500), and there are only two choices per egg.

We can do: start with all to G, then for each egg, decide whether to assign to A.

Greedy: always do it if it doesn't make the difference exceed 500 in absolute value.

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))

# For each egg, calculate the difference if given to A vs G
# If given to A: A gets a, G gets g -> difference A-G = a - g
# If given to G: difference A-G = -g + a = a - g (wait, no)

# Actually, let's define diff_A = sum(a_i for A) - sum(g_i for G)
# If egg i goes to A: adds a_i - g_i to diff_A
# If egg i goes to G: adds 0 - g_i = -g_i to diff_A (but we need to express in terms of a_i)

# Better: if egg i goes to A: contributes (a_i - g_i) to the difference (A-G)
# If egg i goes to G: contributes (0 - g_i) to the difference (A-G) = -g_i
# But since a_i + g_i = 1000, we can write this as:
# -g_i = - (1000 - a_i) = a_i - 1000
# So when assigned to G, contributes (a_i - 1000)
# When assigned to A, contributes (a_i - (1000 - a_i)) = 2*a_i - 1000

# This is messy.

# Alternative: define b_i = a_i - g_i = 2*a_i - 1000
# If given to A: adds b_i to difference (A-G)
# If given to G: adds 0 - g_i = -g_i = -1000 + a_i = (a_i - 1000)
# But a_i - 1000 = (a_i - g_i) - 999? No.

# Actually, if given to G, we contribute -g_i to the difference (A-G)
# Since we want |(sum A) - (sum G)| ≤ 500, we care about (sum A) - (sum G)

# Let's define d_i = a_i - g_i
# If egg i goes to A: difference changes by d_i
# If egg i goes to G: difference changes by -g_i

# But -g_i = -(1000 - a_i) = a_i - 1000

# So differences are d_i = a_i - g_i = 2a_i - 1000
# And for G: contributes a_i - 1000

# The key insight is that when we assign an egg to A, we add (a_i - g_i) to the difference.
# When we assign to G, we add (0 - g_i) = -g_i to the difference.
# But since a_i + g_i = 1000, we can write this as:
# A: + (a_i - g_i)
# G: + (-g_i)

# However, we can reframe: if we assign egg i to A, we get a_i, and G gets nothing from this egg.
# If we assign to G, we get g_i, and A gets nothing.

 n = int(input())
 eggs = []
 for i in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))

 sum_a = sum(a for a, g in eggs)

 k = round(sum_a / 1000)

 # k must be integer in [0, n]
 if k < 0: k = 0
 if k > n: k = n

 # then output the string
 result = []
 for i in range(n):
    if i < k:
        result.append('G')
    else:
        result.append('A')

 print(''.join(result))

 Therefore, the code is: 
 Note: k must be between 0 and n inclusive, so we clamp it.

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
sum_a = 0

for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))
    sum_a += a

k = round(sum_a / 1000)

# Ensure k is in [0, n]
k = max(0, min(k, n))

result = []
for i in range(n):
    if i < k:
        result.append('G')
    else:
        result.append('A')

print(''.join(result))
```

I have double-checked the derivation and it seems to work. The difference after swapping k eggs from A to G is |sum_a - 1000*k|, which is ≤500 by choice of k, and we only need to ensure that k is in [0, n] which we do by clamping.

So this should be the solution. 
Note: in the derivation, when we swap k eggs from A to G, the difference in total cost (S_A - S_G) is exactly (sum_a - 1000*k), and |that| ≤500, so the absolute difference is within 500. 

Yes, and in all examples, it works. 

Final solution:
```python
n = int(input())
sum_a = 0

for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    sum_a += a

k = round(sum_a / 1000)
k = max(0, min(k, n))

result = []
for i in range(n):
    if i < k:
        result.append('G')
    else:
        result.append('A')

print(''.join(result))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The Bitlandians are quite weird people. They have very peculiar customs.

As is customary, Uncle J. wants to have n eggs painted for Bitruz (an ancient Bitland festival). He has asked G. and A. to do the work.

The kids are excited because just as is customary, they're going to be paid for the job! 

Overall uncle J. has got n eggs. G. named his price for painting each egg. Similarly, A. named his price for painting each egg. It turns out that for each egg the sum of the money both A. and G. want for the painting equals 1000.

Uncle J. wants to distribute the eggs between the children so as to give each egg to exactly one child. Also, Uncle J. wants the total money paid to A. to be different from the total money paid to G. by no more than 500.

Help Uncle J. Find the required distribution of eggs or otherwise say that distributing the eggs in the required manner is impossible.


-----Input-----

The first line contains integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^6) — the number of eggs.

Next n lines contain two integers a_{i} and g_{i} each (0 ≤ a_{i}, g_{i} ≤ 1000; a_{i} + g_{i} = 1000): a_{i} is the price said by A. for the i-th egg and g_{i} is the price said by G. for the i-th egg.


-----Output-----

If it is impossible to assign the painting, print "-1" (without quotes).

Otherwise print a string, consisting of n letters "G" and "A". The i-th letter of this string should represent the child who will get the i-th egg in the required distribution. Letter "A" represents A. and letter "G" represents G. If we denote the money Uncle J. must pay A. for the painting as S_{a}, and the money Uncle J. must pay G. for the painting as S_{g}, then this inequality must hold: |S_{a}  -  S_{g}|  ≤  500. 

If there are several solutions, you are allowed to print any of them.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
1 999
999 1

Output
AG

Input
3
400 600
400 600
400 600

Output
AGA### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n eggs, each with two prices: A's price and G's price
2. For each egg, a_i + g_i = 1000 (they always sum to 1000)
3. We need to assign each egg to either A or G
4. The absolute difference between total payments to A and G should be ≤ 500
5. We need to find such an assignment or return -1 if impossible

## Key Insight

For each egg i:
- If we give it to A, we pay a_i to A and 0 to G
- If we give it to G, we pay g_i to G and 0 to A

The total payment to A is sum of a_i for eggs assigned to A.
The total payment to G is sum of g_i for eggs assigned to G.

Since a_i + g_i = 1000, the total sum of all prices is 1000n.

Let's say we assign some eggs to A and the rest to G. If we let S_a be total paid to A and S_g to G, then:
- S_a + S_g = 1000n
- We want |S_a - S_g| ≤ 500

Since S_g = 1000n - S_a, we have:
|S_a - (1000n - S_a)| ≤ 500
|2S_a - 1000n| ≤ 500
-500 ≤ 2S_a - 1000n ≤ 500
1000n - 500 ≤ 2S_a ≤ 1000n + 500
(1000n - 500)/2 ≤ S_a ≤ (1000n + 500)/2

So S_a should be in the range [500n - 250, 500n + 250].

## Strategy

For each egg, we can calculate the "benefit" of assigning it to A vs G:
- Assigning to A: we get "gain" of a_i - g_i = a_i - (1000 - a_i) = 2a_i - 1000
- Assigning to G: we get "gain" of g_i - a_i = 2g_i - 1000 = - (2a_i - 1000)

The "gain" when assigning to A is 2a_i - 1000.

We want to find an assignment where the total gain is within [500n - 250, 500n + 250].

Actually, let me think differently:

The difference between S_a and S_g can be expressed as:
S_a - S_g = sum of (a_i * I_i - g_i * (1 - I_i)) where I_i is 1 if assigned to A, 0 otherwise.

S_a - S_g = sum of (a_i * I_i - g_i * (1 - I_i))
= sum of (a_i * I_i - g_i + g_i * I_i)
= sum of ((a_i + g_i) * I_i - g_i)
= sum of (1000 * I_i - g_i)
= 1000 * (# of eggs assigned to A) - sum of g_i for all eggs

Let k be the number of eggs assigned to A. Then:
S_a - S_g = 1000k - sum(g_i)

We want |S_a - S_g| ≤ 500, so |1000k - sum(g_i)| ≤ 500.

This means: sum(g_i) - 500 ≤ 1000k ≤ sum(g_i) + 500
(sum(g_i) - 500)/1000 ≤ k ≤ (sum(g_i) + 500)/1000

k must be an integer between these bounds.

## Algorithm

1. Calculate total sum of g_i values
2. Find the range of valid k values (number of eggs to assign to A)
3. For each possible k in this range, check if we can achieve it
4. To achieve a specific k, we can use greedy: assign to A when (a_i - g_i) > 0, or when it helps reduce the difference

Actually, simpler approach:
- The difference S_a - S_g = sum of (a_i - g_i) for eggs assigned to A, minus sum of g_i for eggs assigned to G, but this gets complex.

Better approach: use the fact that we can compute the total difference.

For an egg, if assigned to A: contributes (a_i - g_i) to the difference S_a - S_g
If assigned to G: contributes (g_i - a_i) = -(a_i - g_i) to the difference

Let d_i = a_i - g_i = 2a_i - 1000.

If we assign egg i to A, difference increases by d_i.
If we assign to G, difference decreases by d_i.

Total difference = sum of d_i for eggs assigned to A.

We want |total difference| ≤ 500.

So we need to find a subset of eggs such that the sum of d_i for selected eggs is in [-500, 500].

This is a subset sum problem with small target range.

Since |d_i| ≤ 1000, and we only need sum in [-500,500], we can use a greedy or try to find a feasible assignment.

Actually, we can be more direct:

The total difference D = sum of (a_i - g_i) for eggs assigned to A.

Since each a_i + g_i = 1000, we have a_i - g_i = 2a_i - 1000.

We want |D| ≤ 500.

We can start with all eggs assigned to G (difference = 0).
Then for each egg, if assigning it to A increases the difference by (a_i - g_i), and if the result stays within [-500,500], we assign it.

But we need to be careful about the order.

Actually, a simpler approach:
1. Calculate the difference d_i = a_i - g_i for each egg
2. Sort eggs by d_i (descending or ascending)
3. Try to assign eggs to minimize |total difference|

But since n can be up to 10^6, we need an efficient solution.

Alternative insight:
- The minimum possible |difference| is 0, and we can achieve any difference that's achievable through subset selection.
- Since we're only interested in difference in [-500,500], and the step is the d_i values, we can use greedy.

However, there's a simpler observation: we can just distribute the eggs to minimize the total difference. We can try to assign each egg to the one with lower price.

For each egg, if a_i < g_i, then giving to A is cheaper, so assign to A.
If a_i > g_i, then giving to G is cheaper, so assign to G.
If a_i == g_i, either way.

Let's try this greedy assignment first.

With this assignment:
- Total S_a = sum of a_i for eggs where a_i < g_i
- Total S_g = sum of g_i for eggs where a_i > g_i (plus g_i where a_i = g_i)

Wait, let me recalculate:
- For egg where a_i < g_i: assign to A, so S_a += a_i, S_g += 0

So the algorithm is:

1. Calculate T_a = sum of a_i for all i
2. Calculate D = 1000*n - T_a
3. Find if there exists integer k in [0, n] such that |1000k - D| ≤ 500
4. This is equivalent to k in [(D-500)/1000, (D+500)/1000]
5. Find the integer k in this interval and within [0, n]
6. If found, output a string with first k characters as 'A', rest as 'G'
7. If not found, output -1

```python
n = int(input())
eggs = []
T_a = 0

for _ in range(n):
    a, g = map(int, input().split())
    eggs.append((a, g))
    T_a += a

# Calculate D = 1000*n - T_a
D = 1000 * n - T_a

# Find k such that |1000k - D| <= 500
# This means k is in [ (D - 500) / 1000, (D + 500) / 1000 ]
left_bound = (D - 500) / 1000.0
right_bound = (D + 500) / 1000.0

# Find integer k in [left_bound, right_bound] and in [0, n]
valid_k = None

# We can iterate through possible k values
for k in range(0, n + 1):
    if left_bound <= k <= right_bound:
        valid_k = k
        break

if valid_k is None:
    print(-1)
else:
    result = ""
    for i in range(n):
        if i < valid_k:
            result += "A"
        else:
            result += "G"
    print(result)
```
